- **Advisor tool model pairing.** The advisor tool's `model` must be at least as capable as the request's top-level `model` — e.g. executor `claude-sonnet-5` → advisor `claude-opus-4-8` or `claude-opus-4-7`. An invalid pair returns 400. Pairing table in `shared/tool-use-concepts.md` § Advisor. Availability: `shared/platform-availability.md`.
- **Agent Skills ≠ Managed Agents.** To have Claude generate a `.pptx`/`.xlsx`/etc. via Agent Skills, call `client.beta.messages.create` with `container={"skills": [...]}`, the `code_execution_20260521` tool, and both `code-execution-2025-08-25` + `skills-2025-10-02` betas. Do not use `client.beta.agents` / `sessions` / `environments` here — those are the Managed Agents surface, not Agent Skills.
- **MCP connector needs both halves.** `mcp_servers=[{type:"url", url, name}]` alone is rejected as a validation error — also add `tools=[{type:"mcp_toolset", mcp_server_name:<same name>}]` with beta `mcp-client-2025-11-20`. Availability: `shared/platform-availability.md`.
- **`inference_geo` is a direct top-level request parameter** — `client.messages.create(..., inference_geo="us")` / `.inferenceGeo("us")`. Do not put it in `extra_body` / `putAdditionalBodyProperty`. (Messages API only — on Managed Agents, `inference_geo` instead nests inside the agent's `model` object, never top-level; see `shared/managed-agents-core.md` § Pinning inference geography.) Supported on Opus 4.6 / Sonnet 4.6 and later; availability: `shared/platform-availability.md`. `response.usage.inference_geo` reports where inference ran.
- **Fine-grained tool streaming is not a beta feature.** Set `eager_input_streaming: true` on the tool definition and call the regular `client.messages.stream(...)`. There is no beta header and no `client.beta.*` path.
- **Cache diagnostics is beta.** Use `client.beta.messages.*` with beta `cache-diagnosis-2026-04-07`. Pass `diagnostics: {previous_message_id: null}` on the first turn and `diagnostics: {previous_message_id: <previous response id>}` on subsequent turns; the result is on `response.diagnostics`. Availability: `shared/platform-availability.md`.
- **Memory tool type is `memory_20250818`.** Declare `{"type": "memory_20250818", "name": "memory"}`. Go uses the beta-namespace type `{OfMemoryTool20250818: &anthropic.BetaMemoryTool20250818Param{}}` on `client.Beta.Messages.New`; Python/TypeScript/Ruby/PHP/C# use the non-beta `client.messages.create`; Java has both a non-beta `MemoryTool20250818` and a beta tool-runner path. Python/TypeScript provide `BetaAbstractMemoryTool` / `betaMemoryTool` helpers for implementing the backend.
- **Use a model the feature actually supports.** Some features are restricted to specific model tiers — fast mode is Claude Opus 5 / Opus 4.8 only (and Claude API only), task budgets (Messages API only — Managed Agents session budgets have no model-tier restriction) are Claude Opus 5 / Fable 5 / Sonnet 5 / Opus 4.8 / 4.7 only, and the advisor tool requires a valid executor↔advisor pair. If the user's prompt names a model that the feature doesn't support, use a supported model instead and note the substitution in the output.
- **Don't define custom types for SDK data structures:** The SDK exports types for all API objects. Use `Anthropic.MessageParam` for messages, `Anthropic.Tool` for tool definitions, `Anthropic.ToolUseBlock` / `Anthropic.ToolResultBlockParam` for tool results, `Anthropic.Message` for responses. Defining your own `interface ChatMessage { role: string; content: unknown }` duplicates what the SDK already provides and loses type safety.
- **Report and document output:** For tasks that produce reports, documents, or visualizations, the code execution sandbox has `python-docx`, `python-pptx`, `matplotlib`, `pillow`, and `pypdf` pre-installed. Claude can generate formatted files (DOCX, PDF, charts) and return them via the Files API — consider this for "report" or "document" type requests instead of plain stdout text.
- **Server-tool errors don't raise.** Web search and web fetch errors return HTTP 200 with a `web_search_tool_result` / `web_fetch_tool_result` block whose `content` is a single error object (e.g. `{error_code: "max_uses_exceeded"}`) — not a raised exception. For web search, a success `content` is a *list*; an error `content` is an *object* — branch on that before indexing.
- **Code execution output block type:** `code_execution_20260521` returns `bash_code_execution_tool_result` (with `.content.stdout`), **not** the legacy bare `code_execution_tool_result`. Iterate `response.content` and match on the correct type.
- **Tool search: never defer everything.** The search tool itself must not have `defer_loading: true`, and at least one tool in `tools` must be non-deferred, or the API returns 400 `All tools have defer_loading set`.

I've read all four docs. Here's my recommended stack for the six deferred decisions in `docs/requirements.md` §10, plus the pieces the brief demands that aren't yet on the board (guardrails, metrics, eval).

## Recommended stack

| Decision | Pick | Why | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Parsing** | **Docling** (IBM, MIT) | One library for PDF/DOCX/HTML → a structured doc model with real layout, table structure (TableFormer), heading hierarchy, and image extraction. A unified parser is far easier to justify than three per-format libraries stitched together, and its output *is* the chunking input. | Ships torch models → ~2GB image, slow-ish ingest. Ingest is offline so it never touches the 5s budget. |
| **Images inside docs** | Docling extracts them → caption each with **Claude** (vision), index the caption as its own chunk linked to its parent section | This is the "multimodal approach" the brief asks you to justify, and it's the only way an image's content becomes retrievable. Also a strong demo moment. | One-time ingest cost per image. |
| **Vector DB** | **Qdrant** | Official Docker image, single Rust binary, zero external deps (Weaviate needs more wiring, Chroma's hybrid/scale story is weaker). Native **hybrid search** — dense + sparse in one query with server-side fusion — plus payload filtering and deterministic point IDs for upserts. | None meaningful for a PoC. |
| **Embeddings** | **BGE-M3** | Multilingual by design, genuinely strong on German, and *cross-lingual* — a German query retrieves an English passage, which is exactly your 20%-overlap test. One model emits dense **and** sparse vectors, so it feeds Qdrant hybrid from a single forward pass. 8k context. | CPU inference. Fine for 65 docs; blocks the event loop if you're careless (see below). |
| **Chunking** | Structure-aware, ~512 tokens, ~15% overlap, **tables kept whole**, heading-path prepended to every chunk before embedding | Splitting on document structure rather than character count is what makes mixed-layout docs work. Prepending `Doc title › Section › Subsection` to the embedded text ("contextual retrieval, lite") is a large, cheap accuracy win and trivially explainable. | ~nothing. |
| **Re-ranking** | **bge-reranker-v2-m3** cross-encoder — retrieve top-30 hybrid, rerank to top-5 | Same model family as the embedder, so multilingual behaviour is consistent. This is the component your staged 50→+5→+10 corpus exists to demonstrate. | Latency risk on CPU (30 pairs can run 0.5–2s). Make it swappable and **measure** — it's the single biggest threat to the 5s target. |
| **LLM** | **`claude-opus-5`**, streamed via `AsyncAnthropic` | Best grounding and instruction-following; the `effort` parameter is your latency/cost dial without changing models. Note there's no Anthropic embeddings endpoint, so embedder ≠ LLM vendor is unavoidable and worth stating in the write-up. | Per-token cost. |

## LLM integration specifics that will bite you

- **Sampling params are gone.** `temperature`/`top_p`/`top_k` return a 400 on Opus 5. Steer with the prompt.
- **Thinking is on by default** on Opus 5 and `max_tokens` caps thinking *plus* answer. For grounded QA, run `output_config={"effort": "low"}` and keep adaptive thinking on. Do **not** reach for `thinking: {"type": "disabled"}` — on Opus 5 that has documented failure modes (leaked `<thinking>` tags in visible output) and lower effort gets you the same latency saving safely.
- **Use native citations.** Pass retrieved chunks as `document` content blocks with `citations: {"enabled": True}`. The API then returns citation spans tied to source chunks — this converts "high answer accuracy" from a claim into a verifiable artifact, and gives your frontend clickable sources for free. (Incompatible with `output_config.format`, so don't also ask for JSON.)
- **Prompt caching** on the system prompt + guardrail block. Opus 5's minimum cacheable prefix is 512 tokens — low enough that your policy prompt will actually cache. Cheap, and it's a real production-awareness point for the presentation.
- **Handle `stop_reason == "refusal"`** before reading `response.content`, and consider opting into server-side `fallbacks`.

## Guardrails (mandatory per brief, currently unaddressed)

Layered, and each layer is one sentence to justify:

1. **Role separation** — policy lives in the `system` prompt; retrieved content goes in the *user* turn only.
2. **Injection containment** — wrap each chunk in delimiters with an explicit instruction that the enclosed text is untrusted data, never instructions. Retrieved documents are attacker-controllable the moment a customer-supplied PDF enters the corpus.
3. **KB constraint** — "answer only from the provided documents; if they don't contain the answer, say so." Enforce it downstream: if the response carries zero citations, replace it with the fallback message.
4. **Input validation** — already done in `app/routers/api.py:14`.

## Concurrency — the non-obvious risk

Your embedder and reranker are **CPU-bound and synchronous**. Called directly from an `async def` handler they block the event loop and your "concurrent users" requirement quietly fails under load. Run them via `anyio.to_thread.run_sync()` behind a bounded semaphore, or in a separate process. This is the most defensible perf decision in the whole build and worth calling out explicitly.

For metrics: per-stage timers (embed / search / rerank / TTFT / total) in structured JSON logs, plus a `/metrics` endpoint. And redefine the target — with streaming, the number that matters is **time-to-first-token** (aim <1.5s); "completion ≤5s" is the secondary bound.

## Test corpus + accuracy eval (solves two open questions at once)

Generate the 65 docs with Claude in two stages: a structured content plan (topics, EN/DE pairs for the 20% cross-lingual overlap) → render to HTML (Jinja), DOCX (`python-docx`), PDF (WeasyPrint from the HTML), with matplotlib charts as embedded images and real tables.

Have the same generation step emit a **golden Q&A set** — question, expected answer, source doc. That gives you, for free:
- **Retrieval metrics** — recall@k and MRR against known source doc IDs. Deterministic, no LLM needed, and re-runnable across the 50/55/65 corpus stages to *prove* the re-ranking claim.
- **Answer correctness** — LLM-judge with a rubric.
- **Abstention rate** — include deliberately unanswerable questions to test the KB guardrail.
- **Cross-lingual** — German questions over English sources.

Run it as a `pytest -m eval` suite alongside the latency tests already planned in §7.

**Incremental ingestion:** sha256 per document, deterministic chunk UUIDs from `(doc_id, chunk_index)`, upsert into Qdrant, manifest of doc hashes. Unchanged docs are skipped, deleted docs are removed by `doc_id` filter. Covers the staged-batch demo cleanly.

One model emits dense **and** sparse vectors, so it feeds Qdrant hybrid from a single forward pass. 8k context. | CPU inference. Prepending `Doc title \u203a Section \u203a Subsection` to the embedded text (\"contextual retrieval, lite\") is a large, cheap accuracy win and trivially explainable. | ~nothing. |\n| **Re-ranking** | **bge-reranker-v2-m3** cross-encoder \u2014 retrieve top-30 hybrid, rerank to top-5 | Same model family as the embedder, so multilingual behaviour is consistent. LLM integration specifics that will bite\n\n- **Sampling params are gone.** `temperature` / `top_p` / `top_k` return a 400 on\n  Opus 5. Steer with the prompt instead.\n- **Thinking is on by default** on Opus 5, and `max_tokens` caps thinking *plus*\n  answer. For grounded QA, run `output_config={\"effort\": \"low\"}` and keep\n  adaptive thinking on. Do **not** reach for `thinking: {\"type\": \"disabled\"}` \u2014\n  on Opus 5 that has documented failure modes (leaked `<thinking>` tags in\n  visible output), and lower effort gets the same latency saving safely.\n- **Use native citations.** Pass retrieved chunks as `document` content blocks\n  with `citations: {\"enabled\": True}`. Opus 5's minimum\n  cacheable prefix is 512 tokens \u2014 low enough that the policy prompt will\n  actually cache. Cheap, and a real production-awareness point for the\n  presentation.\n- **Handle `stop_reason == \"refusal\"`** before reading `response.content`, and\n  consider opting into server-side `fallbacks`.\n\n## 3. Guardrails (mandatory per brief, currently unaddressed)\n\nLayered, and each layer is one sentence to justify:\n\n1. **Role separation** \u2014 policy lives in the `system` prompt; retrieved content\n   goes in the *user* turn only.\n2. **Injection containment** \u2014 wrap each chunk in delimiters with an explicit\n   instruction that the enclosed text is untrusted data, never instructions.\n   Retrieved documents are attacker-controllable the moment a customer-supplied\n   PDF enters the corpus.\n3. **KB constraint** \u2014 \"answer only from the provided documents; if they don't\n   contain the answer, say so.\" Enforce it downstream: if the response carries\n   zero citations, replace it with the fallback message.\n4. **Input validation** \u2014 already done in `app/routers/api.py`.\n\n## 4. Concurrency \u2014 the non-obvious risk\n\nThe embedder and reranker are **CPU-bound and synchronous**. Called directly\nfrom an `async def` handler they block the event loop, and the \"concurrent\nusers\" requirement quietly fails under load. Run them via\n`anyio.to_thread.run_sync()` behind a bounded semaphore, or in a separate\nprocess. This is the most defensible perf decision in the whole build and worth\ncalling out explicitly.\n\n**Metrics:** per-stage timers (embed / search / rerank / TTFT / total) in\nstructured JSON logs, plus a `/metrics` endpoint.\n\n**Redefine the target:** with streaming, the number that matters is\n**time-to-first-token** (aim <1.5s); \"completion \u22645s\" is the secondary bound.\n\n## 5. Test corpus + accuracy eval (solves two open questions at once)\n\nGenerate the 65 docs with Claude in two stages: a structured content plan\n(topics, EN/DE pairs for the 20% cross-lingual overlap) \u2192 render to HTML\n(Jinja), DOCX (`python-docx`), PDF (WeasyPrint from the HTML), with matplotlib\ncharts as embedded images and real tables.\n\nHave the same generation step emit a **golden Q&A set** \u2014 question, expected\nanswer, source doc. That gives, for free:\n\n- **Retrieval metrics** \u2014 recall@k and MRR against known source doc IDs.\n  Deterministic, no LLM needed, and re-runnable across the 50/55/65 corpus\n  stages to *prove* the re-ranking claim.\n- **Answer correctness** \u2014 LLM-judge with a rubric.\n- **Abstention rate** \u2014 include deliberately unanswerable questions to test the\n  KB guardrail.\n- **Cross-lingual** \u2014 German questions over English sources.\n\nRun it as a `pytest -m eval` suite alongside the latency tests planned in\nrequirements \u00a77.\n\n## 6. Incremental ingestion\n\nsha256 per document, deterministic chunk UUIDs from `(doc_id, chunk_index)`,\nupsert into Qdrant, manifest of doc hashes. Unchanged docs are skipped, deleted\ndocs are removed by `doc_id` filter. Covers the staged-batch demo cleanly.\n\n## 7. Open forks (decide before building)\n\n### A. Where should the embedding + re-ranking models run?\n\nThe real trade is *reviewer experience* (small image, fast `docker compose up`,\nguaranteed latency) versus *narrative consistency* (\"local vector DB, local\nmodels, one API key\"). Whether reranker latency actually threatens the 5s\nbudget is measurable, not knowable up front.\n\n| Option | Upside | Downside |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Local** (BGE-M3 + bge-reranker) \u2014 *leaning this way* | No second API vendor, no extra keys, fully offline retrieval, consistent \"local\" story end-to-end | ~2\u20133GB Docker image, CPU inference, reranker latency is the main risk to the 5s target |\n| **API** (Voyage/Cohere embed + rerank) | Small fast image, sub-100ms embed and rerank, latency target basically guaranteed | Second vendor and API key to manage, network dependency, \"local\" now applies only to the vector DB |\n| **Hybrid** (local embeddings, API rerank) | Keeps embeddings offline (the bulk of the work) while removing the component most likely to blow the latency budget | More moving parts to justify |\n\n### B. How much should the document parser handle?\n\nDocling's cost is mostly a heavier image and slow *offline* ingest \u2014 it never\ntouches the 5s completion target. The lightweight path is faster to build but\nweakens exactly the \"tables & images requiring careful processing\" requirement\nthe brief calls out.\n\n| Option | Upside | Downside |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Docling, all three formats** \u2014 *leaning this way* | One unified parser with real table structure and layout awareness; strongest justification story; best table/mixed-layout quality | Heavy image, slow offline ingest |\n| **Lightweight per-format** (PyMuPDF4LLM + python-docx + BeautifulSoup) | Tiny image, fast, no ML models at ingest | Noticeably weaker on tables and complex layouts \u2014 the exact thing the brief calls out |\n| **Docling + Claude vision on every page** | Highest fidelity on tricky layouts | Most expensive and slowest ingest by a wide margin |\n\n### Still up for revisiting\n\nThe LLM config (\u00a72), the guardrail layering (\u00a73), the eval design (\u00a75), and\noverall scope.\n"

## Cross-cutting (decide first — it constrains 2, 3, 6)

**1. Where do the ML models run: local or API?**
Applies to embeddings and re-ranking (not the LLM, which is API by mandate). Drives Docker image size, install experience, latency budget, key management, and whether "local" describes just the vector DB or the whole retrieval path.

## The five the brief explicitly says to pick *and justify*

**2. Document understanding technology** — unified parser vs per-format libraries vs multimodal; and specifically how tables and embedded images become retrievable.

**3. Embedding model** — must handle EN + DE, and cross-lingual if you want the 20%-overlap corpus to prove anything.

**4. Chunking strategy** — unit, size, overlap, what happens to tables, and whether chunks carry context beyond their own text.

**5. Vector DB** — local, Docker-image-shipping. Also: dense-only or hybrid, since that's really a DB capability question.

**6. LLM + how it's called** — model choice, plus the configuration that actually determines behaviour: effort/thinking, citations, caching, streaming.

## Mandatory in the brief, no decision recorded anywhere yet

**10. What the 5s target actually means** — with streaming, time-to-first-token and total completion are different numbers. Picking which one you're defending changes the design.

**11. Concurrency model** — how request handling stays parallel when parts of the pipeline are CPU-bound and synchronous.

**12. API key and secret management** — mandatory in the brief, currently an untouched bullet in `work.md`.

**13. Conversation history** — does the API accept prior turns, and does history feed the retrieval query, the prompt, both, or neither? Already flagged in `work.md`; it changes the `/api/chat` contract, so it's cheaper to decide before the contract hardens.

---

Suggested order: **1 → 2 → 5 → 3 → 4 → 7 → 6 → 8 → 9/14 → 10/11 → 12 → 13.**

Rationale: #1 unblocks the most, and #14 is worth pulling forward next to #9 because if corpus generation also emits the golden Q&A set, the accuracy question mostly answers itself.

For #1 to be decidable, the useful thing is that "the ML models" isn't one thing — it's ~8 distinct model invocations, and they split cleanly by whether they sit in the request path.

## Ingest time — runs once per document, latency irrelevant

| # | Stage | Model type | Local / API | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Page layout analysis | Object detection over page images (DocLayNet-class) | **Local only** | Bundled inside the parser. Doesn't exist if you go lightweight per-format. |
| 2 | Table structure recognition | Specialised table model (e.g. TableFormer) | **Local only** | Separate model from #1. This is what turns a table into rows/cells rather than jumbled text. |
| 3 | OCR | Text recognition | **Local** (Tesseract/EasyOCR/RapidOCR) | **Only needed for scanned PDFs.** Your corpus is generated, so digital-native — arguably not needed at all. Real-world hedge vs. dead weight. |
| 4 | Image / chart understanding | Vision-language model | **Genuine fork** | Claude vision (API) or a small local VLM. Without this, embedded images are unretrievable. |
| 5 | Chunk embedding (bulk) | Text embedding | **Genuine fork** | Few thousand chunks for 65 docs. Must be the *same model* as #7. |
| 6 | Sparse/lexical vectors | BM25 **or** learned sparse (SPLADE / BGE-M3 sparse head) | Local either way | BM25 needs no model at all — worth separating from the learned option. |

## Query time — inside the 5s / TTFT budget

| # | Stage | Model type | Local / API | Latency stakes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | Query embedding | Text embedding (same model as #5) | **Genuine fork** | One short string. Local CPU ~20–50ms; API ~50–150ms round trip. Roughly a wash. |
| 8 | Query sparse vector | Same as #6 | Local | Negligible. |
| 9 | **Re-ranking** | Cross-encoder over ~30 query–doc pairs | **Genuine fork** | **The one that matters.** Local CPU plausibly 0.5–2s; API ~100–200ms. This is the only local model that seriously threatens the budget. |
| 10 | Generation | LLM | **API — mandated by brief** | Dominates the budget regardless. |
| 11 | *(optional)* Query preprocessing | Language detection / translation / rewriting | Either, or none | Probably unnecessary — a cross-lingual embedder is precisely the argument for not needing this. |
| 12 | *(optional)* Injection classifier | Small classifier or LLM call | Either | Buys guardrail depth, costs latency. Likely skip for a PoC. |

## Offline / not in the product

| # | Stage | Model | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 13 | Test corpus generation | LLM (API) | Repo dependency, not runtime. |
| 14 | Eval judge | LLM (API) | Retrieval metrics (recall@k, MRR) need no model — pure arithmetic. |

---

## What this means for the decision

**Only three of these are actually a choice: #4 (image captioning), #5/#7 (embeddings), #9 (re-ranking).** Everything else is either forced API (generation, judge) or forced local (parser-internal models — which exist only if you pick an ML-based parser).

**Two couplings worth knowing before you answer #1:**

- **#1 is partly decided by #2.** If the parser is ML-based, you're shipping torch and several hundred MB of models *anyway*. At that point a local embedder is cheap marginal cost. If the parser is lightweight, a local embedder is what drags torch into the image in the first place — and the image-size argument flips completely.
- **Embedding and re-ranking are separable.** They're usually bundled into one "local vs API" answer, but only re-ranking has real latency exposure. Splitting them is a legitimate third position, not a fudge.

One thing worth noting on the hidden coupling: even if embeddings go API-side, chunk sizing (#4 in the requirements) still needs that model's **tokenizer** locally, or you're guessing at token counts.

Rough image-size shape if everything goes local — treat as estimates to verify, not commitments: torch CPU ~0.9GB installed, embedder ~1–2.2GB, reranker ~1–2.2GB, parser models a few hundred MB. So realistically **3–5GB**.

Where do you want to push first — the latency exposure on #9, or the image-size/install-experience side?

## user — 11:40

that's a lot of models. What would be a minimal amount of models for this task?

## assistant — 11:40

Good instinct — most of that list is optional, and the honest floor is much lower than it looks.

## The floor

**One model: Claude.** Everything else has a non-ML substitute:

- Parsing → pure-code libraries (PyMuPDF, python-docx, BeautifulSoup). No ML.
- Retrieval → BM25. Pure statistics, no model, no weights.
- Images → caption with the LLM you already have.
- Re-ranking → drop it.

But BM25-only retrieval fails your own requirement: a German query against an English passage has near-zero lexical overlap, so the 20% cross-lingual corpus retrieves nothing. It also loses paraphrase matching, which is most of why RAG beats keyword search. So one model is the floor, not the answer.

## Recommended minimum: **2 models**

| Model | Jobs it does |
|---|---|
| **Multilingual embedder** | Chunk embedding + query embedding |
| **Claude** | Generation, image captioning (vision, at ingest), eval judge, test-corpus generation |

The collapse comes from one observation: **Claude is already in the stack and covers four separate roles.** Every job you hand it is a model you don't ship. Add BM25 for the lexical half of hybrid search — zero models, and Qdrant does it server-side — and you're at two.

## What each dropped model actually costs

| Dropped | Real cost | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|
| Layout detection | Weaker structure on complex PDF layouts | **DOCX and HTML tables are already structured** — no ML was ever needed for 2 of your 3 formats. Only PDF is genuinely hard. |
| Table structure ML | Ruled-table extraction in PDF gets worse | `pdfplumber` handles ruled tables acceptably; your generated PDFs can be rendered from HTML, so they'll be clean |
| OCR | Can't read scanned documents | Your corpus is digital-native by construction. Document as a known limitation — it's an honest one |
| Local VLM | — | Claude vision does it better, at ingest, with zero image weight |
| Cross-encoder reranker | Retrieval precision on the larger corpus | See tension below |

## The one real tension

`requirements.md` §3 says the staged +5/+10 batches exist *specifically* to demonstrate re-ranking. Dropping it contradicts a decision you've already recorded.

Three ways out, in increasing cost:

1. **Demonstrate it offline, not in the request path** — show recall@k improving with re-ranking in the eval suite. Proves the concept, costs zero request latency, ships zero weights.
2. **API reranker** (Cohere Rerank) — ~100ms, zero local weight, one more vendor.
3. **Local cross-encoder** — back to a third model and the latency risk.

Option 1 is the one I'd argue for: it satisfies the requirement as *evidence* rather than as a runtime feature, which fits a PoC whose brief explicitly values reasoning over feature completeness.

## Footprint, minimal stack

The local/API axis is independent of model count:

| Variant | Local models | Rough image size |
|---|---|---|
| API embeddings + Claude | **0** | ~200MB (FastAPI + Qdrant client) |
| Local small embedder (e5-small class) + Claude | **1** | ~1.5GB |
| Local large embedder (BGE-M3 class) + Claude | **1** | ~3GB |

So the minimal-model answer and the minimal-footprint answer aren't the same question — you can be at two models and still ship nothing locally.

Worth saying plainly: given the brief weights "code quality, architectural reasoning, clear explainable decisions" over feature completeness, a deliberately small stack with documented trade-offs is a stronger submission than a maximal one — provided you can say *why* each thing isn't there. "We don't ship OCR because the corpus is digital-native, and here's what would change if it weren't" is a better answer than shipping OCR you never exercise.

Two things to settle next: whether the embedder goes local or API, and whether you accept option 1 for re-ranking. Which first?

## assistant — 12:22

Recorded. Both are well-reasoned — "start smallest, upgrade only on evidence" is exactly the kind of decision the brief wants you to be able to defend, and the Cohere exclusion has an honest justification (trial-tier limits make a reviewer's `docker compose up` unreproducible, plus a second API key to manage).

Four consequences that change downstream work:

**1. e5 requires input prefixes — and this is the classic silent failure.** `multilingual-e5-small` expects `query: ` on queries and `passage: ` on indexed chunks. Omitting them, or mismatching between ingest and query, degrades retrieval substantially with no error. Worth a unit test that asserts the prefix on both paths.

**2. It caps chunk size at 512 tokens, which constrains decision #4.** That ceiling includes the prefix *and* the heading-path prepend. My earlier "~512 tokens" suggestion sat right at the limit — with e5-small it should come down to roughly 380–420 to leave headroom. Chunking is now partly decided by this choice.

**3. Your upgrade trigger needs a measurement to fire on.** "Update to larger models only if it's not good enough" is the right policy, but with re-ranking deferred, retrieval quality now rests entirely on e5-small + BM25 — and nothing currently tells you whether it's good enough. That makes the eval harness (#9) a prerequisite for your own upgrade decision, not a nice-to-have. Recall@k on the golden set is the number that fires the trigger.

**4. Plan the swap now, because it's not in-place.** e5-small is 384-dim; e5-large and BGE-M3 are 1024. A dimension change means recreating the Qdrant collection, not migrating it. Cheap to design for, expensive to retrofit:
- embedder behind an interface, model name + dimension recorded in the ingest manifest
- re-index as a single command

One option worth knowing: Qdrant supports **multiple named vectors per point**, so you could hold e5-small and BGE-M3 embeddings side by side in one collection and A/B them on the same corpus without a second ingest. That turns "compare if there's time" into an afternoon rather than a rebuild.

Also: keep the sparse half as **BM25 regardless**. BGE-M3 would bundle its own sparse vectors, but if the hybrid layer assumes that, swapping models breaks two things at once. Independent sparse keeps the embedder swap clean.

Two follow-ups:

- **Still open inside #1:** image captioning. You've settled embeddings, but not whether embedded images get described by Claude vision at ingest or are simply skipped. That's the last local-vs-API fork in the ML list.
- **Doc inconsistency to fix:** `requirements.md` §3 justifies the staged +5/+10 corpus batches partly by "re-ranking — retrieval quality as the corpus grows." With re-ranking deferred, the batches still earn their place via incremental ingestion, but that second justification needs re-scoping.

Want me to write these decisions into `docs/requirements.md` §10 (following your existing `**Decision — X:**` + strikethrough convention), or into `stack-proposal.md`?

## user — 16:45

let's talk about dealing with the documents. what tools are available?

## assistant — 16:46

Before the inventory, one framing point that determines which tools are even viable.

## What the parser has to emit

The parse→chunk boundary is the real architectural decision here. Two options:

- **A Markdown string.** Simple, but page numbers, element types, and positions are gone. You then can't cite "page 7 of the returns policy" — which weakens the citation story that grounding depends on.
- **A structured element list** — `[{type: heading|paragraph|table|image, text, page, section_path, doc_id}]`. Chunking gets to make decisions per element type (keep tables whole, attach captions to images), and citations get real provenance.

I'd argue for the second, and it eliminates some tools immediately: anything that only emits Markdown is a downgrade you can't recover from later.

## The landscape

### Per-format, pure code (no ML)

| Format | Tool | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| PDF | **pdfplumber** | Best-in-class ruled-table extraction, word-level coordinates, page numbers for free. Slower. MIT. |
| PDF | **PyMuPDF / pymupdf4llm** | Fastest by a wide margin, good text + image extraction, Markdown output. **AGPL** — see licensing below. |
| PDF | **pypdf** / **pdfminer.six** | Basic text; pypdf has no real layout analysis. Fine as a fallback, not a primary. |
| DOCX | **python-docx** | The standard. **Tables are natively structured — no ML needed.** Gotcha: paragraphs and tables are separate collections, so reading the body *in document order* means dropping to `document.element.body` XML. |
| DOCX | **docx2python** | Also pulls headers, footers, footnotes — which python-docx handles poorly. |
| HTML | **BeautifulSoup + lxml** | Full control, tables natively structured. |
| HTML | **trafilatura** | Strips boilerplate/nav to main content. Excellent — but it *discards* what it judges to be chrome, which is a risk when the whole page is the document. |

The observation worth sitting with: **DOCX and HTML carry real table structure already.** ML parsing buys you nothing there. PDF is the only genuinely hard format, because a PDF table is just ink that happens to line up.

### Unified / ML document understanding

| Tool | Coverage | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Docling** (IBM) | PDF, DOCX, HTML, PPTX, XLSX | Layout model + TableFormer, structured `DoclingDocument`, tokenizer-aware chunker, optional VLM captioning and OCR. MIT. Heavy — but you're already shipping ~2GB for BGE-M3, so the marginal cost is lower than it looks. |
| **Unstructured** | Very broad | `partition()` returns typed elements (Title, NarrativeText, Table, Image) — a good structural fit. Its strong PDF path (`hi_res`) pulls in heavy vision deps. |
| **Marker** | PDF → Markdown | High quality, but **commercial-use restrictions** — verify before using in a submission. |
| **MinerU** | PDF | Strong, **AGPL**. |
| **LlamaParse**, Azure Document Intelligence, AWS Textract | Cloud APIs | Rule these out — they conflict with the local/reproducible posture and add vendor keys. |

### LLM-as-parser

Render each page to an image, send to Claude vision, get structured output back. Handles mixed layout, tables, *and* image content in a single pass, with zero local ML weight. Costs API calls per page and slow ingest — for 65 docs at up to 25 pages that's potentially ~1000 pages, so it's real but bounded and one-time. Viable as a **fallback for pages where deterministic parsing fails**, which is a more defensible design than using it for everything.

## Licensing — worth checking now, not at submission

**PyMuPDF is AGPL-3.0.** It's the fastest and most tempting PDF library, and for a take-home going to a company, AGPL is a conversation you probably don't want. pdfplumber (MIT), python-docx (MIT), BeautifulSoup (MIT) and Docling (MIT) are clean. Treat my license notes as "verify these," not gospel — they shift.

## One trap specific to your setup

You generate the corpus yourself. There's a quiet incentive to generate documents that are easy to parse, which makes the parser look good and proves nothing. The brief explicitly asks for "mixed layouts" and "embedded information that requires careful processing" — so the corpus needs multi-column pages, tables that span page breaks, unruled tables, and charts that only exist as images. **Decide corpus difficulty and parser capability together**, or you'll accidentally set your own exam.

## The three axes to decide

1. **Unified vs. per-format** — one library for all three, or the best tool per format behind a common interface?
2. **ML vs. pure code for PDF** — this is really the only place ML is in question, given DOCX/HTML are structured already.
3. **What happens to images** — still open from the ML inventory, and it interacts: Docling can caption locally, Claude can caption via API, or you skip them.

Which axis do you want to take first? My instinct is #2, since it's the one that actually decides the image size and the ingest complexity.

## user — 16:58

we'll do unified (docling) for all types. Also try to do the images by creating captions for them so we can reference the captions - using docling

## assistant — 16:58

Good — and Docling makes that coherent, because captions land in the same `DoclingDocument` tree as everything else, so an image caption keeps its section context instead of floating free.

Two bonuses worth knowing:

- **Docling's `HybridChunker` is tokenizer-aware** — point it at BGE-M3's tokenizer and it respects structure while packing to a token target. That partially answers decision #4 before we get to it.
- **Turn OCR off explicitly.** Your corpus is digital-native, so OCR is pure ingest cost. Docling enables it in some default configurations; disabling it is both a speedup and a documentable decision ("no scans by construction").

## The sub-decision this creates: which VLM writes the captions?

This is where the value actually lives, and it's easy to get wrong. Captioning is only useful if the caption contains *retrievable facts*. Compare:

- `"a bar chart"` — indexed, retrieved by nobody, pure cost
- `"Bar chart of return rates by product category for Q3, showing electronics highest at 12%"` — actually answers a support question

Docling's local picture-description default is a very small VLM (SmolVLM-256M class), which reliably produces the first kind. The larger local option (Granite Vision ~2B class) produces the second, at several GB. Docling also supports pointing picture description at an **external VLM API** — but note that path is built around OpenAI-compatible endpoints, so wiring it to Claude is an adapter, not a config flag.

| Captioner | Caption quality | Added image size | Ingest speed |
|---|---|---|---|
| SmolVLM-class (Docling default) | Poor — generic | ~0.5GB | Fast |
| Granite-Vision-class (local) | Good | ~4–6GB | Slow on CPU |
| Claude vision via API | Best | 0 | Fast, costs per image |

Treat those specifics as "verify against the Docling version you pin" — its API and defaults have moved quickly.

## The thing to watch: total image size

This is the one that could hurt the reviewer experience you've prioritised since §4:

```
BGE-M3            ~2GB
Docling layout + TableFormer   ~0.5GB
VLM captioner     0 – 6GB   ← the swing factor
torch (CPU)       ~1GB
```

All-local with a good captioner realistically lands at **8GB+**. That's a long `docker compose up` for someone reviewing your submission. Mitigation either way: bake model weights into the image at build time so first run is offline and doesn't download at startup.

## Two things to verify early

1. **Does picture description apply to DOCX/HTML images, or only PDF?** Docling's picture-description options hang off the PDF pipeline; whether images embedded in DOCX/HTML flow through the same captioning path needs checking. If they don't, "unified for all types" has a gap precisely where you're depending on it.
2. **Cache captions by image hash.** 65 docs will have a few hundred images; at CPU VLM speeds that's plausibly 30–60 minutes of ingest. Re-running that on every iteration will make development miserable — and hash-keyed caching fits the incremental-ingestion design you already want.

---

The captioner choice is genuinely a fork: it's the difference between a ~2.5GB image with weak captions, a ~8GB image with good ones, or a small image with an API dependency. Which way do you want to go — and shall I record the Docling decision into the three docs once you've picked, or now with the captioner left open?

## user — 17:04

we will not turn ocr off anymore. picture description for all doc types (if missing alt). For images goal would be to determine if they have a description and only create one if there's none. we'll use docling's default for starters

## assistant — 17:04

Noted on all three. One upside of keeping OCR on that's worth capturing as justification: it reads text *inside* images — chart axis labels, legends, numbers baked into a diagram — which the captioner won't reliably transcribe. So OCR and captioning complement each other on exactly the figures you care about, rather than OCR being dead weight.

## The alt-text-first strategy — where the existing description lives

Per format, the thing you're checking for:

| Format | Where an authored description lives | Availability |
|---|---|---|
| HTML | `<img alt="...">` | Reliable — and you control it in the generated corpus |
| DOCX | `wp:docPr` `descr` / `title` attributes (the accessibility fields) | Present if the author filled them in |
| PDF | `/Alt` in the tagged structure tree; otherwise the nearby "Figure 3: …" caption line | Often absent — PDF is where the VLM will actually earn its keep |

**Verify early:** does Docling *surface* those fields on its picture items? Its document model is built around layout-derived structure, and DOCX `descr` / HTML `alt` are metadata that sit outside that. If Docling doesn't expose them, you'll need to pull them yourself from the source file and join on image position — which is doable but is real work, and your whole "only caption if missing" design depends on it. Worth a 20-minute spike before building around it.

**Add a quality gate on "has a description."** Alt text in the wild is frequently `image1.png`, `chart`, or empty. Treat a description as *present* only if it's non-trivial — say, above a length threshold and not filename-shaped — otherwise you'll skip captioning precisely where the alt text is useless.

**Record provenance on every caption** — `authored` vs `generated`. Cheap to store, and it lets the eval answer "are generated captions actually helping retrieval, or adding noise?", which is the evidence that would justify upgrading off the default VLM later.

## One trap, same shape as the corpus one

You generate the corpus *and* its alt text. If every image gets good alt text, the VLM path never fires, never gets tested, and you can't demonstrate it. Deliberately leave a portion of images — especially in the PDFs — without alt text, so the fallback is genuinely exercised and you have something to show.

Using Docling's default VLM to start is consistent with the reasoning you applied to e5 vs BGE-M3: cheapest thing that could work, upgrade on measured evidence. And the alt-text-first design makes it more defensible, since the weak captioner only runs on the minority of images that lack a description.

Want me to write the parsing decisions — Docling unified, OCR on, alt-text-first captioning with Docling's default VLM — into `requirements.md`, `considerations.md` and `work.md` now?

Chosen over per-format libraries (pdfplumber / python-docx / BeautifulSoup) and over cloud document-AI services:\n  - one library and one output model for PDF/DOCX/HTML means one code path to build, test and explain, instead of three parsers behind a shared interface\n  - produces a **structured document tree** (headings, paragraphs, tables, images with page/section provenance), not a flat Markdown string \u2014 this is what makes table-aware chunking and page-level citations possible later\n  - includes layout analysis and dedicated table-structure recognition, which is what PDF needs (DOCX and HTML already carry table structure natively)\n  - cloud services (LlamaParse, Azure Document Intelligence, Textract) rejected: they conflict with the local/reproducible posture and add another vendor and key\n  - trade-off accepted: ships ML models, so a larger image and slower ingest. Ingest is offline, so it never touches the 5s completion target\n- **Decision \u2014 OCR: enabled.** Although the generated corpus is digital-native (\u00a73) and therefore does not *need* OCR for page text, OCR reads text rendered *inside* images \u2014 chart axis labels, legends, numbers baked into diagrams \u2014 which the captioner does not reliably transcribe. It complements image captioning on exactly the figures that matter, and keeps the pipeline honest against real-world scanned input.\n- **Decision \u2014 images: alt-text-first captioning.** Every image should end up with a text description that is indexed and referenceable:\n  - first use an **authored** description if one exists \u2014 HTML `alt`, DOCX `descr`/`title` accessibility fields, PDF `/Alt` or a nearby \"Figure N:\" caption\n  - only if none exists (or it is trivial \u2014 empty, filename-shaped, or below a length threshold) generate one with a **vision model**\n  - applies to **all three formats**, not just PDF\n  - start with **Docling's default picture-description model**; upgrade only on measured evidence, consistent with the embedding-model reasoning in \u00a75.2\n  - record **provenance** (`authored` vs `generated`) on each description, so the evaluation (\u00a77) can answer whether generated captions help or add noise\n- Open: verify that Docling surfaces authored alt/`descr` metadata on its picture items. The alt-text-first design depends on it; if it does not, that metadata has to be extracted from the source file separately and joined on image position."
}

  "new_string": "## Document corpus\n\nAll documents are digitally generated, no scanned docs.\n\nConsequence: OCR is not strictly required for page text. Scanned-document\nhandling is a documented limitation rather than a silent omission \u2014 a real\ncustomer-support corpus would likely contain scans. (OCR is switched on\nanyway, for a different reason \u2014 see \"OCR \u2014 kept on\" below.)\n\n### Generating the corpus \u2014 the self-set-exam trap\n\nWe author both the corpus and the pipeline that reads it. The obvious failure\nmode is generating documents that happen to be easy for our own parser, which\nmakes the pipeline look good and proves nothing. The brief explicitly asks for\n\"mixed layouts\" and \"embedded information that requires careful processing\",\nso the corpus has to be *deliberately* hard:\n\n- multi-column and mixed-layout pages, not just linear prose\n- tables that span a page break, and unruled tables (no cell borders) as well\n  as ruled ones \u2014 an unruled table is where PDF table extraction actually gets\n  tested\n- charts and diagrams that exist **only as images**, carrying information that\n  appears nowhere in the surrounding text, so image handling is load-bearing\n  rather than decorative\n- a deliberate portion of images left **without** authored alt text \u2014 otherwise\n  the alt-text-first design below means the generated-caption path never fires,\n  never gets tested, and can't be demonstrated\n- documents long enough (up to 25 pages) to exercise chunking and section\n  context\n\nThe same principle applies to the golden Q&A set: questions whose answers live\nonly in a table or only in a chart image are the ones that prove the pipeline\nworks.\n\n## Document parsing\n\n### Docling, unified across all three formats\n\nAlternatives considered: best-of-breed per format (pdfplumber + python-docx +\nBeautifulSoup), other unified parsers (Unstructured, Marker, MinerU), cloud\ndocument-AI (LlamaParse, Azure Document Intelligence, Textract), and\nLLM-as-parser (render pages to images, send to Claude vision).\n\n- **Cloud document-AI \u2014 rejected.** Conflicts with the local/reproducible\n  posture and adds another vendor and API key.\n- **Per-format libraries \u2014 rejected.** Genuinely defensible: DOCX and HTML\n  already carry table structure natively, so ML parsing buys nothing there, and\n  PDF is the only hard format. But it means three parsers, three sets of edge\n  cases and three things to explain, all behind a hand-written common\n  interface. Also worth noting on licensing: PyMuPDF, the fastest PDF option,\n  is AGPL \u2014 awkward for a submission. pdfplumber (MIT) would have been the\n  pick.\n- **Docling \u2014 chosen.** One library and one output model for all three formats.\n  Critically, it emits a **structured document tree** (headings, paragraphs,\n  tables, images, with page and section provenance) rather than a flat Markdown\n  string \u2014 that provenance is what makes table-aware chunking and page-level\n  citations possible. Layout analysis plus dedicated table-structure\n  recognition covers the PDF case. MIT licensed.\n- **Trade-off accepted:** ships ML models, so a bigger image and slower ingest.\n  Ingest is offline, so it never touches the 5s completion target.\n- **Bonus:** Docling's `HybridChunker` is tokenizer-aware and can be pointed at\n  BGE-M3's tokenizer, which likely settles the chunking decision too.\n\n### OCR \u2014 kept on\n\nThe corpus is digital-native, so OCR isn't needed for page text and switching\nit off would be a free speedup. Kept on anyway because OCR reads text rendered\n*inside* images \u2014 chart axis labels, legends, numbers baked into diagrams \u2014\nwhich the captioner does not reliably transcribe. OCR and captioning\ncomplement each other on exactly the figures that matter.\n\n### Images \u2014 alt-text-first captioning\n\nGoal: every image ends up with an indexed, referenceable text description.\nPrefer a description a human already wrote; only generate one when there isn't\none.\n\nWhere an authored description lives, per format:\n\n| Format | Source | Reliability |\n|---|---|---|\n| HTML | `<img alt=\"...\">` | Reliable |\n| DOCX | `wp:docPr` `descr` / `title` accessibility fields | Present if the author filled them in |\n| PDF | `/Alt` in the tagged structure tree, or a nearby \"Figure N:\" caption | Often absent \u2014 PDF is where generation earns its keep |\n\n- **Quality gate:** treat a description as present only if it is non-trivial.\n  Alt text in the wild is frequently empty, `image1.png`, or `chart` \u2014 skipping\n  generation on those would skip it exactly where it is most needed.\n- **Provenance:** record `authored` vs `generated` per description. Cheap, and\n  it lets the evaluation answer whether generated captions actually help\n  retrieval or just add noise \u2014 which is the evidence that would justify\n  upgrading the captioner.\n- **Model:** start with Docling's default picture-description model. It is\n  small and its captions are generic (\"a bar chart\") rather than factual\n  (\"return rates by product category, electronics highest at 12%\"), and only\n  the latter is retrievable. Accepted for now on the same reasoning used for\n  the embedding model: cheapest thing that might work, upgrade on measured\n  evidence. The alt-text-first design also means it only runs on the minority\n  of images that lack a description.\n- **Cache captions by image hash.** A few hundred images at CPU vision-model\n  speeds is plausibly 30\u201360 minutes of ingest; re-running that every iteration\n  would make development miserable, and hash-keyed caching fits the incremental\n  ingestion design anyway.\n\n**To verify:** whether Docling surfaces authored alt/`descr` metadata on its\npicture items at all. Its document model is layout-derived, and that metadata\nsits outside the layout. If it doesn't expose them, the values have to be\npulled from the source file separately and joined on image position \u2014 doable,\nbut real work that the whole alt-text-first design depends on.\n\n### Image size\n\nModel weights stack up, and a slow `docker compose up` works directly against\nthe smooth-install priority in requirements.md \u00a74:\n\n    BGE-M3                        ~2GB\n    Docling layout + table models ~0.5GB\n    OCR engine                    small\n    picture-description VLM       0.5GB (default) .. 6GB (larger local model)\n    torch (CPU)                   ~1GB\n\nMitigation regardless of the final numbers: bake model weights into the image\nat build time so first run is fully offline and doesn't download at startup.\n\n## Retrieval stack"
}

All three docs updated.

**`requirements.md`**
- §3 — added two decisions: *corpus difficulty* (multi-column pages, tables spanning page breaks, unruled tables, charts existing only as images, a deliberate share of images without alt text, docs long enough to stress chunking) and *corpus is digital-native*.
- §5.1 — the three parsing decisions with rationale: Docling unified (with per-format libraries and cloud document-AI recorded as rejected, and why), OCR enabled with the reads-text-inside-images justification, and alt-text-first captioning across all formats including the quality gate, provenance flag, and default-model-for-now reasoning. Left one explicit open item: verifying Docling actually surfaces authored alt metadata.
- §10 — parsing and image-captioning questions struck through as resolved. Chunking is now the only remaining ingestion question, annotated with the `HybridChunker` + BGE-M3 tokenizer lead.

**`considerations.md`** — new *Document parsing* section holding the rationale and the rejected alternatives (including the PyMuPDF AGPL note), the alt-text source table per format, the caption-caching and provenance reasoning, and a model-weight size breakdown. The corpus section gained the self-set-exam trap and what "deliberately hard" concretely means.

**`work.md`** — parsing decision struck through; corpus tasks now carry the difficulty requirements; ingestion section gained the Docling spike, the captioning flow, quality gate, provenance, and caption caching as discrete tasks.

One item is worth treating as a real blocker rather than a note: **the Docling alt-metadata spike**. The captioning design assumes Docling exposes HTML `alt` and DOCX `descr` on its picture items, and that metadata sits outside the layout model Docling is built around. If it doesn't, you're extracting it from source files yourself and joining on image position — a meaningfully different piece of work. Cheap to check now, expensive to discover mid-build.

Next in the order we set is the vector DB (#5) — now a two-horse race between Qdrant and Weaviate, since BGE-M3's sparse vectors rule out anything without hybrid support.

Based on the search results, I found relevant information about alt text extraction in the context of document processing:

In DOCX files, the "descr" attribute is alternative text used for assistive technologies which do not display the picture. You can extract this information using an XPath query targeting `//w:drawing//wp:docPr` to access the `descr` attribute from Word documents.

The search results also show that Docling includes a PictureItem class as part of its document element types, which would be the structure used to represent images in Docling's document model.

For HTML output, the alt attribute is used in HTML documents to specify alternative text that is to be displayed in place of an element that cannot be rendered. The extraction from DOCX to HTML would involve converting the `descr` attribute from the Word document's picture properties into the standard HTML `alt` attribute.

The search results show references to Docling's reference documentation, but they don't contain detailed information about how Docling specifically handles the extraction of alt text from DOCX files and conversion to HTML. If you need more specific technical details about Docling's implementation, you may want to check Docling's official documentation or GitHub repository directly.

Based on the search results, here's what I found about Docling picture description annotations and PictureDescriptionData in docling-core:

## Overview

Picture description uses Vision Language Models (VLMs) to annotate pictures with textual descriptions. Enrichment models operate on the assembled DoclingDocument after the document structure has been built from backend output, and they process document elements in batches to add metadata such as picture classifications, textual descriptions, extracted chart data, and code/formula annotations.

## PictureDescriptionData Structure

Descriptions are added as PictureDescriptionData to PictureItem.annotations, and the PictureDescriptionData structure requires a description string at minimum.

## API Integration

Docling expects the external API's response for image descriptions to closely match the OpenAI API format, specifically returning a top-level field with the image description text (usually called "description").

## Usage Example

PictureDescriptionData is imported from docling_core.types.doc.document, and can be accessed via pic.meta.description to retrieve the created_by and text fields.

The search results indicate that PictureItem from docling_core/types/doc/document.py can have multiple annotation types, with annotations stored directly in the annotations list.

**Result**
```
# PictureItem Fields and Accessibility Metadata

Based on the documentation, **PictureItem** has these key attributes:

- `annotations` - For storing classification data
- `captions` - Text descriptions associated with the image
- `image` - The actual image reference
- `label` - Item classification
- `prov` - Provenance/source information
- `comments` - Associated comments
- `references` - Cross-references to other items

## Accessibility Support

PictureItem supports **captions** as its primary mechanism for providing descriptive text. The class includes a `caption_text` property that extracts caption content.

However, the API reference does **not explicitly list** dedicated fields for:
- `alt_text`
- `alt_description`
- `accessibility_metadata`

## Available Annotations

PictureItem supports `PictureClassificationData`, which contains:
- `predicted_classes` - Classification results with confidence scores
- `kind` - Classification type identifier
- `provenance` - Source of the classification

The `get_annotations()` method retrieves these classification details, but this addresses image categorization rather than textual accessibility descriptions.

**In summary**: PictureItem relies on the `captions` field for descriptive metadata, not a dedicated alt-text attribute.
```

# Image Handling in the HTML Backend

## Search Results

The code **does not read the `alt` attribute** from `img` tags. Here's the evidence:

### Image Emission
The primary method that adds pictures is `_emit_image()`. While the full implementation is truncated in the provided content, the method is called like this:

```python
if name == "img":
    _flush_buffer()
    im_ref3 = self._emit_image(node, doc)
```

### Alt Text Status
Searching through the visible code reveals **no references to the `alt` attribute**. The code processes various HTML attributes (like `href`, `hidden`, `aria-hidden`, `style`, `id`, `class`, `start`, `data-docling-id`), but alternative text is never extracted or passed to the document model.

### Picture Addition
While `add_picture()` calls aren't shown in full in the truncated section, the pattern suggests images are added as `PictureItem` objects from `docling_core.types.doc`, which are imported at the top. The absence of alt-attribute handling in the visible preprocessing and attribute extraction code indicates **alt text is discarded**.

The implementation focuses on rendering coordinates, dimensions, and image data itself, but deliberately omits accessibility metadata like alt descriptions.

Best Practices for Image Descriptions | TestParty","url":"https://testparty.ai/blog/alt-text-best-practices"}]

Based on the web search results, I found several relevant issues in the docling-project repository related to alt text, accessibility, and image description extraction:

## Key Issues Found:

**Issue #2446** - When converting images using Docling, the library does not generate picture descriptions for image formats. The pictures=[] array remains empty for all images, regardless of whether they contain text or not, making it impossible to retrieve any visual content descriptions. Notably, if the same image is embedded in a PDF file, Docling correctly generates picture descriptions and populates the pictures array.

**Issue #2225** - The image description option is not available for docx and ppt file formats, though it is available for PDF processing.

**Discussion #2581** - Related to vision-language model (VLM) image descriptions, there are configuration issues where VLM-based image descriptions (from Ollama/Llava) do not appear in markdown output, only a placeholder comment, due to how Docling's markdown serializer prioritizes PDF captions over VLM-generated annotations.

These issues highlight significant gaps in Docling's ability to extract and provide accessibility features for image descriptions across different file formats, which is particularly important for accessibility compliance.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```
</details>

Checked, and the design assumption doesn't hold. Two separate problems, not one.

## Finding 1 — Docling discards authored alt text

- **`PictureItem` has no field for it.** Its fields are `label`, `annotations`, `image`, `prov`, plus `captions` / `references` / `footnotes` inherited from `FloatingItem`. There's no `alt_text` or accessibility field — so even if a backend read it, there'd be nowhere to put it.
- **The HTML backend never reads `alt`.** It handles `href`, `hidden`, `aria-hidden`, `style`, `id`, `class`, `start` — `alt` is not among them.
- **The DOCX backend never reads `docPr` / `descr`.** `_handle_pictures()` processes blip elements only; the accessibility attributes aren't touched.

The only text that reaches a picture is `captions` (visible caption text found near the figure by layout analysis) and `annotations` (VLM-generated descriptions).

## Finding 2 — the bigger one: picture description is PDF-only

[Issue #2225](https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2225) reports the image-description option isn't available for DOCX and PPTX, only PDF. [Issue #2446](https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2446) reports the same for standalone image files — `pictures=[]` stays empty, while the identical image embedded in a PDF describes correctly.

So **"picture description for all doc types"** isn't currently supported by Docling's pipeline, independent of the alt-text question.

Confidence: high on direction, moderate on specifics. I read `main`, and issue status can change — pin a Docling version and confirm with a short script against one DOCX and one HTML before committing.

## What this does to the decision

Both halves of "picture description for all doc types, if missing alt" are blocked:

| Assumption | Reality |
|---|---|
| Detect existing alt/`descr` | Not exposed — discarded at the backend |
| Caption whatever lacks it, all formats | Captioning runs on PDF only |

## The way out

Do image handling as **your own pipeline step**, using Docling for *extraction* rather than description:

1. Docling parses the document and gives you `PictureItem`s with `image` bytes and page/section provenance — this works across formats; only *description* is PDF-limited.
2. You separately pull authored alt from the source file — `alt` from HTML via BeautifulSoup, `descr` from `wp:docPr` in the DOCX XML.
3. Join the two on **image content hash** (hash Docling's extracted bytes and your own). Robust against ordering differences; duplicate images collide, which is acceptable.
4. Caption anything still undescribed with a captioner **you** call.

This inverts a constraint into an advantage: once captioning is yours, the model choice is free — Docling's default VLM, or Claude vision, without the OpenAI-compatible-endpoint adapter Docling's API path would require.

Cheaper alternative worth considering: **give the corpus visible figure captions** ("Figure 3: Return rates by category"). Docling *does* extract caption text into `captions` across formats via layout analysis, so this sidesteps both problems with no extra code — and it's realistic, since technical documentation usually captions its figures. The catch is it's partly authoring around the tooling, and it won't help for images that genuinely have no visible caption.

Worth verifying before you build either: that Docling actually populates `image` bytes for DOCX and HTML pictures. Issue #2446 makes me want that confirmed rather than assumed.

Want me to update the three docs with this finding and whichever approach you pick?

Sources: [Docling document reference](https://docling-project.github.io/docling/reference/docling_document/) · [html_backend.py](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/docling-project/docling/main/docling/backend/html_backend.py) · [msword_backend.py](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/docling-project/docling/main/docling/backend/msword_backend.py) · [docling-core document.py](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/docling-project/docling-core/main/docling_core/types/doc/document.py) · [Issue #2225](https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2225) · [Issue #2446](https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2446) · [Pictures description example](https://docling-project.github.io/docling/_generated/examples/pictures_description/)

All three docs updated to caption-first.

**`requirements.md`** §5.1 — images decision is now: use `PictureItem.captions` when present, generate only for pictures without one, accessibility metadata explicitly out of scope. §3 corpus decision now says most figures carry a visible caption, with a deliberate share left uncaptioned so the generation path still fires.

**`considerations.md`** — the images section is now caption-first, and records *why* alt-text was abandoned (backends discard `alt`/`descr`; `PictureItem` has no field for it) so the reasoning survives rather than the option looking un-considered.

**`work.md`** — the alt-metadata spike is struck through as answered, replaced by the `PictureItem.image` spike; captioning tasks reworded; provenance is now `extracted`/`generated`.

Two things carried forward from the research that still affect the build, since they're independent of the alt-text question:

1. **Caption generation has to be your own pipeline stage.** Docling's built-in picture description appears to run for PDF only ([#2225](https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2225), [#2446](https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2446)), so relying on its enrichment would silently leave DOCX and HTML images undescribed. The upside is that the captioner model becomes a free choice rather than one constrained by Docling's OpenAI-shaped API integration.

2. **The remaining spike is `PictureItem.image` on DOCX/HTML** — whether you can actually get image bytes out for non-PDF formats. If that's also PDF-only, generation can't run there at all and the design needs rethinking again. It's a ten-line script against one DOCX and one HTML, and worth doing before writing the ingestion module.

Next in the order we set: vector DB (#5), now Qdrant vs Weaviate.

Based on the search results, here's what I found about Weaviate's hybrid search capabilities:

## Weaviate Hybrid Search Overview

Hybrid search in Weaviate combines the results of a vector search and a keyword (BM25F) search by fusing the two result sets, with configurable fusion methods and relative weights.

## Sparse and Dense Vectors

Sparse embeddings are generated from algorithms like BM25 and SPLADE. Dense embeddings are generated from machine learning models like GloVe and Transformers.

## Key Features

Hybrid search merges dense and sparse vectors together to deliver the best of both search methods. 

Weaviate's single-call hybrid() with an alpha parameter offers a simple API for hybrid search. Additionally, Weaviate Hybrid Search 2.0 (October 2025) features learned fusion that trains a small model on query patterns to predict optimal weights dynamically.

## Support for Multiple Models

The search results indicate that SPLADE or BGE-M3 can be used as single models for both sparse and dense representations. This allows developers to bring their own embedding models to Weaviate's hybrid search infrastructure.

The system is designed to be flexible, allowing you to configure both the sparse retriever (BM25 or SPLADE) and the fusion method based on your specific use case needs.

Based on the search results, here's information about Qdrant sparse vectors, BGE-M3, hybrid query, prefetch, fusion, and RRF:

## Hybrid Search with Qdrant

In text search, it is often useful to combine dense and sparse vectors to get the best of both worlds: semantic understanding from dense vectors and precise word matching from sparse vectors.

## BGE-M3 Support

The BAAI/bge-m3 model supports both dense and sparse vectors, and sparse vectors from SPLADE or BGE-M3 are learned sparse representations that capture semantic similarity, not just term overlap.

## Prefetch and Fusion Architecture

The main component for making the combinations of queries possible is the prefetch parameter, which enables making sub-requests. Prefetches can have prefetches themselves, so you can have nested prefetches.

## Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF)

RRF considers the positions of results within each query and boosts those that appear closer to the top in multiple sets of results. When sparse returns fewer results (or none), dense search fills the gaps, and documents ranked highly by both methods get boosted in RRF scores.

The search results include practical examples of implementing hybrid search with Qdrant using prefetch operations with RRF fusion to combine results from both dense and sparse vector searches.

# Weaviate Sparse Vectors Question

**The Question:** Can Weaviate accept user-provided sparse vectors for hybrid search, or does it only generate its own BM25 inverted index?

**The Official Answer:** Based on the forum post, Weaviate automatically handles both components during data ingestion. The response indicates that "when you ingest/update data, Weaviate will vectorize your data" and "create all the necessary inverted index for BM25 search."

The post doesn't explicitly address whether users can supply pre-computed sparse vectors. Instead, it emphasizes that Weaviate's automatic process generates both the dense vector embeddings and the BM25 keyword index needed for hybrid search—suggesting the platform manages sparse vector creation internally rather than accepting external sparse vectors.
```
</details>

Verified, and it's decisive. Weaviate's hybrid search **cannot accept your own sparse vectors** — the keyword side is always its internal BM25F inverted index over object properties. The `hybrid()` parameters are `query`, `vector`, `alpha`, `bm25_operator`, `query_properties`; there is no slot for a precomputed sparse vector. Qdrant treats sparse vectors as a first-class type and fuses them server-side via the Query API's `prefetch` + RRF/DBSF.

## The comparison

| | **Qdrant** | **Weaviate** |
|---|---|---|
| Custom sparse vectors | ✅ First-class | ❌ Internal BM25F only |
| Hybrid mechanism | `prefetch` sub-queries + RRF/DBSF fusion, one request | `alpha` knob blending dense + BM25F |
| Language | Rust, single binary | Go |
| Image / memory | Lighter | Heavier |
| Embedded mode (no Docker) | ✅ `:memory:` or local path | ✖ (limited/deprecated — verify) |
| Named vectors | ✅ | ✅ |
| Config to first insert | Collection + vector params | Collection schema, `vectorizer: none` |
| Point IDs | **unsigned int or UUID only** | UUID |
| Extras | Quantization, snapshots | Multi-tenancy, RBAC, replication, vectorizer modules |
| License | Apache 2.0 | BSD-3 |

## Why this settles it for your stack

You chose BGE-M3 *specifically* because it emits dense and sparse from one model, so you'd never hand-roll a separate lexical path. Weaviate discards exactly that half. You'd be running BGE-M3 dense + Weaviate's BM25 — which works, but the justification you recorded in §5.2 evaporates.

And there's a concrete quality argument beyond consistency: **BM25 handles German badly.** German compounds mean a query for *Rücksendung* won't match *Rücksendebedingungen* — they're different tokens to a lexical index, absent language-specific stemming and decompounding you'd have to configure. BGE-M3's sparse vectors are *learned* and multilingual, so they match on semantics rather than surface form. Given EN/DE is a hard requirement, that's not a small difference.

Two more that matter for your specific setup:

- **Embedded mode.** Qdrant runs in-process against a local path or `:memory:`, so your `pytest` suite tests real retrieval without Docker — consistent with how your e2e suite already avoids heavyweight infrastructure.
- **Named vectors.** This is what makes the "compare BGE-M3 against a smaller embedder if time allows" task in `work.md` an afternoon rather than a second ingest.

## Being fair to Weaviate

It's the more feature-complete product — multi-tenancy, RBAC, replication are real, and its `alpha` knob is more ergonomic than composing prefetch clauses. None of that is load-bearing for a single-user PoC with 65 documents, and the extra surface area is more to configure and explain.

## One gotcha if you pick Qdrant

Point IDs must be **unsigned integers or UUIDs** — not arbitrary strings. Your deterministic chunk-ID scheme (`doc_id` + `chunk_index`) needs hashing into a UUID; `uuid5` over that tuple is the standard move and stays deterministic, so upsert-based incremental ingestion works as designed.

## Recommendation

**Qdrant** — driven by the BGE-M3 decision rather than by general superiority.

Worth naming the coupling explicitly, because it's the interesting architectural point: **the DB and embedder choices are linked.** If you preferred Weaviate, the coherent stack would be Weaviate + BM25 + a *dense-only* embedder — at which point e5-small becomes attractive again at a quarter of BGE-M3's size, and you'd revisit §5.2. Picking Weaviate while keeping BGE-M3 is the one combination that doesn't hang together.

Want me to record Qdrant into the three docs?
