<details><summary>tool_use: WebFetch — Describe HybridChunker: what parameters does it take (tokenizer, max_tokens, ...</summary>

```json
{
  "url": "https://docling-project.github.io/docling/concepts/chunking/",
  "prompt": "Describe HybridChunker: what parameters does it take (tokenizer, max_tokens, merge_peers)? What does the contextualize() method do? How does it handle headings, tables, and captions? Does the chunk carry metadata like headings, page numbers, and doc_items provenance?"
}
```

**Result**
```
# HybridChunker Overview

## Parameters

The `HybridChunker` accepts a **tokenizer** parameter (aligned to your embedding model) and a **max_tokens** limit. It provides a `merge_peers` parameter (default `True`) that users can disable to skip the merging phase. Additional parameters include `repeat_table_header` and `omit_header_on_overflow` for managing table structure across chunks.

## Contextualize Method

The `contextualize()` method performs metadata enrichment. It returns "the potentially metadata-enriched serialization of the chunk, typically used to feed an embedding model (or generation model)."

## Handling Document Elements

The chunker operates through a two-pass approach on hierarchical results:

1. **Splitting pass**: Breaks oversized chunks based on token count
2. **Merging pass**: Combines undersized successive chunks sharing identical headings and captions (unless opted out)

Headers and captions are automatically attached as relevant document metadata. For tables specifically, headers can be repeated at the start of each chunk when spanning multiple sections, ensuring structural context is preserved.

## Metadata Preservation

Chunks retain associated metadata including headings, captions, and document item provenance, though the provided content doesn't explicitly detail the complete metadata structure for page numbers and other attributes.
```
</details>

Useful — Docling already covers more of this than I assumed, so the decision surface is smaller than expected.

## What `HybridChunker` gives you for free

- `tokenizer` — point it at BGE-M3's tokenizer so token counts are real, not estimated
- `max_tokens` — the size target
- Two-pass: split oversized chunks, then **merge undersized peers that share headings/captions** (`merge_peers`, default on)
- `repeat_table_header` and `omit_header_on_overflow` — a table too big for one chunk splits by rows with its header repeated. That's exactly the policy I was going to propose, already implemented
- `contextualize()` — returns the **metadata-enriched serialization** for feeding the embedder. This is the heading-path prepend, built in
- Chunks retain headings, captions, and `doc_items` provenance

So: use `contextualize()` output for embedding, keep raw text for display, and don't hand-roll table splitting.

## The three decisions actually left

### 1. `max_tokens` — the only genuinely open number

BGE-M3 allows 8k, so the model isn't the constraint. Retrieval quality is. A single vector over a long passage averages across topics and matches nothing strongly — precision falls as chunks grow, regardless of what the model *permits*.

**Recommend ~512 tokens** for the embedded unit. Small enough to stay topically coherent, large enough that a paragraph isn't shredded. Since re-ranking is deferred, precision at retrieval time is doing all the work — which argues for the smaller end.

### 2. Retrieval unit vs. generation unit — the real design fork

The classic tension: small chunks retrieve precisely but hand the LLM fragments; big chunks ground well but retrieve poorly. You can have both, because Docling chunks know their parent section.

| Approach | How | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| **Same unit for both** | Retrieve chunk → send chunk | Simplest. Fragments may lack context |
| **Parent expansion** | Retrieve chunk → send its parent section | Best grounding. Needs parent stored + a token cap |
| **Neighbour expansion** | Retrieve chunk → send chunk ± 1 sibling | Middle ground, trivial with `chunk_index` |

Given no re-ranking, I'd argue for **neighbour expansion**: it recovers most of the grounding benefit for roughly no complexity, since you already have deterministic `chunk_index` ordering from the Qdrant ID scheme. Parent expansion is the stronger version if you want to spend the complexity.

### 3. Sequencing — caption generation must happen *before* chunking

This one's an ordering trap rather than a preference. The chunker attaches **captions** to chunks. So generated captions have to be written back into the `DoclingDocument` *before* chunking, or images that had no visible caption produce chunks with nothing in them and the generation step is wasted.

```
parse → generate missing captions into the doc → chunk → contextualize → embed
```

Get this backwards and you'll be bolting image chunks on afterwards by hand.

## Metadata to store per chunk

Needed for citations, filtering, and the eval harness:

`doc_id` · `chunk_index` · `page_no(s)` · `heading_path` · `element_types` (has_table / has_image) · `lang` · `source_format` · `caption_provenance` (extracted/generated) · `doc_content_hash`

`lang` is worth carrying even though EN and DE share one collection — cross-lingual retrieval is the point, but you'll want to slice eval results by language to see whether German is underperforming.

## One thing to verify

Whether `HybridChunker` emits **pictures as their own chunks**, or only attaches captions to surrounding text chunks. If it's the latter, an image whose information appears nowhere in the text — exactly the case your corpus is designed to include — may not be independently retrievable. Worth checking with the same spike as the `PictureItem.image` question.

The open call is **#2**: same-unit, neighbour expansion, or parent expansion?

## user — 18:08

parent expansion for starters, we'll adjust based on size, number of docs later

## assistant — 18:08

Good — and it pairs well with one thing already in the stack: Claude's native citations give span-level precision *within* whatever you send, so sending a whole parent section doesn't cost you citation precision. You get broad grounding context and narrow attribution at once.

Three consequences to design around:

1. **Dedupe parents.** If your top-5 chunks include three from the same section, naive expansion sends that section three times. Dedupe by `parent_id` before building the prompt — otherwise you burn context and skew the model toward one section.
2. **Cap parent size, with a fallback.** A section in a 25-page doc can be enormous. Set a token ceiling; if the parent exceeds it, fall back to chunk ± neighbours. Otherwise a single fat section blows the latency budget.
3. **Don't store parent text in the Qdrant payload.** A section repeated across its ten child chunks is ten copies. Store `parent_id` in the payload and keep parent text in a side store keyed by `(doc_id, section_id)` — you already need a manifest for incremental ingestion, so extend that.

Two things worth flagging as you move on:

**The spike list is now three items**, and they're all cheap and all load-bearing on ingestion:
1. Does Docling populate `PictureItem.image` for DOCX/HTML?
2. Does `HybridChunker` emit pictures as their own chunks?
3. Do the picture-description limitations in #2225/#2446 still hold in the version you pin?

All three are one script against one DOCX, one HTML, one PDF. If #2 comes back negative, your image-only charts aren't independently retrievable and the corpus design assumption in §3 breaks — worth knowing before writing ingestion.

**§10 is down to two:** the LLM (model plus effort/citations/caching/streaming config), and the accuracy harness. The harness is the one with dependencies stacked on it — re-ranking, captioner quality, and BGE-M3's size all now have "revisit if the eval says so" attached.

## assistant — 18:26

Recorded — one honest caveat on the spikes, then the Haiku specifics that will actually bite.

**The spike deferral has one asymmetry worth knowing:** two of the three fail *silently* rather than with errors. If `HybridChunker` doesn't emit pictures as their own chunks, nothing throws — images just never surface in retrieval. Same if `PictureItem.image` is empty for DOCX/HTML: you get zero captions, no exception. So "wait for errors" won't catch those two; **the eval will**, via questions whose answers live only in a chart. That's already in the corpus spec, so the safety net exists — it just moves the detection point from a spike to the eval run. Fine, as long as the eval happens before you'd call ingestion done.

## Haiku specifics that differ from what we discussed

I'd earlier suggested `effort: "low"` as the latency dial. **That doesn't exist on Haiku 4.5** — `output_config.effort` errors there. Concretely:

| | Haiku 4.5 (`claude-haiku-4-5`) | If you later move to Sonnet 5 / Opus 5 |
|---|---|---|
| Thinking | `{"type": "enabled", budget_tokens: N}` or `{"type": "disabled"}` | `{"type": "adaptive"}` — `budget_tokens` returns 400 |
| `effort` | **Errors** | `low`–`max` |
| `temperature` | **Allowed** | Returns 400 |
| Context | 200K | 1M |
| Prompt-cache minimum | **4096 tokens** | 512 (Opus 5) |
| Price /MTok | $1 / $5 | $3/$15 or $5/$25 |

Three consequences:

1. **`temperature` is available on Haiku and not on the newer models.** For the brief's "predictable, consistent response quality" requirement, that's a real lever you'd lose on upgrade — worth knowing you're using a capability that doesn't survive the swap.
2. **Prompt caching may silently no-op.** Haiku 4.5's minimum cacheable prefix is 4096 tokens; a system prompt plus guardrails plausibly lands under that. It won't error — `cache_creation_input_tokens` just stays 0. Verify by asserting `cache_read_input_tokens > 0` rather than assuming.
3. **Isolate the model config.** Haiku→Sonnet/Opus isn't a string swap: you'd drop `temperature`, and swap `budget_tokens` for adaptive thinking plus `effort`. Same "design for the swap" instinct you applied to the embedder — keep it in one place.

On whether Haiku will be good enough: for grounded, cited, extractive answers over retrieved context, it usually is — the hard part of RAG is retrieval, not generation, and you've spent your complexity budget there. Where I'd expect the trigger to fire is **multi-hop synthesis across several chunks** and **nuanced German phrasing**, not simple extraction. Worth making sure the eval set contains both, or you won't detect the ceiling.

Assert `cache_read_input_tokens >\n  0` rather than assuming.\n- Keep model configuration in one place so the swap is a config change, not a\n  hunt through the codebase.\n\n## Verification spikes \u2014 deferred\n\nSeveral open questions about Docling's behaviour (whether `PictureItem.image`\npopulates for DOCX/HTML, whether `HybridChunker` emits pictures as their own\nchunks, whether the PDF-only picture-description limits still hold in the\npinned version) are deliberately **not** investigated up front. We react if\nproblems surface.\n\nThe caveat worth being explicit about: two of these fail **silently**. If\npictures aren't emitted as chunks, nothing throws \u2014 images simply never appear\nin retrieval results. So error monitoring won't catch them; the evaluation will,\nvia the golden questions whose answers exist only inside a chart image. That\nmakes the eval the real safety net for this deferral, which is fine as long as\nit runs before ingestion is considered finished.\n\n## Retrieval stack\n\n### Vector DB \u2014 Qdrant"

## assistant — 18:33

A few, in rough priority order.

## 1. Build a vertical slice before deciding anything else

Every architectural decision is now made and nothing is built — `retrieve()` and `stream_completion()` are still mocks. That's the biggest risk in the project right now, and it grows with every additional decision made on paper.

Suggest: **one document, end to end.** Ingest a single PDF → Qdrant → hybrid query → neighbour expansion → Haiku → stream into the existing UI. No corpus, no eval, no polish.

That does three things at once: proves the chain works, surfaces the deferred Docling spikes for free (you'll see immediately whether pictures become chunks), and converts the remaining open questions from speculation into observation. It also means that if time runs out, you have a working system rather than an excellent plan.

Your existing mock seams are already in the right places, which makes this cheap.

## 2. Don't generate 65 documents yet

The corpus spec is genuinely demanding — multi-column layouts, tables spanning page breaks, unruled tables, charts-as-images, EN/DE at 50/50, 20% cross-lingual overlap, plus a golden Q&A set. That's a meaningful engineering project on its own, and **it isn't the thing being assessed.**

Suggest **5–8 documents** that cover every hard case at least once, build against those, and scale to 65 late — generation is parameterised anyway, so scaling is a loop, not a rewrite. The staged +5/+10 batches still work; you just start from a smaller base.

## 3. Two mandatory items are being skipped for cheap ones

The brief marks these as required, and both are single unelaborated bullets in `work.md`:

- **Prompt guardrails** — explicitly mandatory, and the injection surface is real once retrieved documents enter the prompt.
- **API key / secret management** — explicitly mandatory, "no hardcoded credentials."

Both are hours, not days, and both are directly graded. They're better value per hour right now than any remaining optimisation.

## 4. Two contract decisions to make *before* building the endpoint

Cheap now, annoying later:

- **`retrieve()` must return more than `list[str]`.** Citations, neighbour expansion, and provenance all need chunk metadata. The current mock signature will have to change — decide the chunk shape before writing the real one.
- **Conversation history** (#13 from the original list, still open). It changes `POST /api/chat`'s request body and whether history feeds the retrieval query. Deciding it after the contract hardens means reworking the frontend too.

## 5. You're closer to the documentation deliverable than you think

The brief requires "architecture overview, key design decisions and trade-offs." `considerations.md` — with its rejected options and the reasons — is already most of that. The Weaviate sparse-vector finding and the alt-text dead end are exactly the kind of thing that reads well in a presentation, because they show investigation rather than assertion.

Worth keeping that in mind as you build: the record you've been maintaining *is* a deliverable, not overhead.

## Suggested order

1. Vertical slice, one document
2. Guardrails + secret management
3. Small corpus (5–8 docs) + golden Q&A set from the same generator
4. Eval harness — which then unlocks all four "upgrade on evidence" decisions
5. Metrics, latency and concurrency tests
6. Scale corpus to 65, run the staged-batch demo
7. Docs and packaging

Happy to start on the vertical slice, or to take the guardrails design first if you'd rather keep deciding while the shape is fresh.

Both recorded. Now — conversation history.

It's really three separable decisions that usually get conflated.

## 1. Where history lives

| | |
|---|---|
| **Stateless — client sends prior turns** | No session store, no Redis, horizontal scaling free, mirrors the Anthropic API's own model |
| Server-side session store | Server controls truth, but needs storage — an in-memory dict breaks the moment you run multiple workers |

**Recommend stateless.** A session store is a whole new component for a PoC that explicitly doesn't need auth.

**But note the consequence for the guardrail decision you just made:** if the client supplies history, then *the entire history is user input*, including assistant turns — a caller can forge what "you" previously said. So validation applies to every turn, not just the newest, and you need a cap on turn count and total length. That's the direct cost of stateless, and it's cheap to handle as long as it's deliberate.

## 2. Does the LLM see history

Yes — the brief says "conversational AI chatbot," and follow-ups like *"and in German?"* are table stakes. This part is nearly free.

Two practical details:

- **Drop prior turns' retrieved documents.** Keep the conversation text, but only the *current* turn's retrieved chunks go in the prompt. Otherwise context grows by ~5 chunks per turn and you'll hit both latency and cost problems quickly.
- **Prompt ordering for caching:** system → history → retrieved docs → question. Retrieved docs change every turn, so anything after them is uncacheable; keeping them late preserves the stable prefix. (With Haiku's 4096-token cache minimum, this may not activate at all — worth measuring rather than assuming.)

## 3. Does history affect *retrieval* — the real question

The classic failure: *"Do you ship to Germany?"* → *"What about returns?"* The second query embedded alone retrieves nothing useful.

| Option | Cost | Quality |
|---|---|---|
| Latest message only | Free | Breaks on follow-ups |
| Concatenate last N turns into the query | Free | Noisy — mixes topics, drags in stale terms |
| LLM rewrites history + latest into a standalone query | An extra serial round-trip before retrieval starts | Best |

The rewrite is the quality answer but it's **serial** — it must finish before retrieval begins, so it lands directly on time-to-first-token. On Haiku that's maybe a few hundred ms, affordable but not free.

**Recommend conditional rewriting:** skip it entirely when history is empty. First turns — the majority of requests — pay nothing, and only genuine follow-ups take the hit.

## What I'd propose

Stateless history, capped and fully validated; LLM sees prior turns but only current-turn documents; conditional query rewrite for follow-ups.

One eval consequence worth deciding at the same time: **a single-turn golden Q&A set will never test any of this.** If history is in scope, the eval set needs some multi-turn cases — otherwise the follow-up path ships untested.

Or, the legitimate alternative: **single-turn only**, declared as a scope boundary. It's cheaper, and the brief never explicitly requires multi-turn. Given you're trying to wrap the plan, that's a defensible call.

## assistant — 18:47

Worth flagging one thing that changes: if the rewrite runs for **query improvement generally**, not just follow-up resolution, then it's unconditional — every request pays a serial LLM round-trip before retrieval even starts. Two LLM calls per request, both on the critical path to first token. Affordable on Haiku, but it needs to be in the latency budget deliberately.

That raises one risk and one opportunity:

**Risk:** query rewriting can *hurt* retrieval. A rewrite that "improves" a query sometimes drifts from user intent and retrieves worse than the raw text would have.

**Opportunity:** you don't have to choose. Qdrant's `prefetch` already fuses multiple branches, so you can search on **both** the original and the rewritten query and let RRF sort it out — dense(original) + sparse(original) + dense(rewritten). Extra DB work is negligible next to the LLM call you're already paying for, and it makes the rewrite additive rather than a replacement.

Also useful: the rewrite call can use **structured outputs** to guarantee it returns just a query string. That doesn't conflict with citations on the generation call, since they're separate requests — citations and `output_config.format` are only incompatible within one call.

| | |
|---|---|

| Parsing | Docling unified, OCR on |

| Embeddings | BGE-M3 |

## assistant — 18:54

Recording. One wrinkle worth naming, since it cuts against the fallback plan:

**Your TTFT is no longer small.** With the unconditional rewrite, first token arrives only after `rewrite (LLM call) → embed → hybrid search → neighbour expansion → generation starts`. In a plain chatbot TTFT is nearly free; here it carries most of the pipeline. So if total completion looks bad, TTFT may not be the rescue metric — it'll be inflated by the same serial work.

Which is the argument for **per-stage timers** rather than just two endpoints. If total is disappointing, "here's exactly where the 4.2s goes, and here's the two levers that move it" is a far stronger answer than picking a friendlier headline number. It also makes the deferred re-ranking decision defensible with data.

Which one is emphasised depends on what the data supports.\n- **Note \u2014 TTFT is not cheap in this design.** Because the query rewrite is unconditional and serial (\u00a75.4), first token only arrives after `rewrite \u2192 embed \u2192 hybrid search \u2192 neighbour expansion \u2192 generation`. Recorded in §6.2 and `work.md`.

That leaves §10 with **one** open question — the accuracy evaluation — plus the concurrency model (#11), which is small but shapes the perf tests: the embedder runs on CPU and will serialise requests if called directly from an async handler, so it needs a thread pool with a bounded semaphore. That's arguably a `work.md` task rather than a decision.

Worth noting for the presentation: the per-stage breakdown does double duty. It's the honest answer on latency, *and* it's the evidence base for three of the deferred decisions — whether re-ranking's cost is affordable, whether the rewrite call earns its place, and whether BGE-M3 on CPU is the bottleneck. One instrumentation job, several arguments supported.

Plan's essentially wrapped. Whenever you want to pick it back up, the two remaining threads are the eval design and the vertical slice.

Written to be the single place a person or\n> an AI agent reads to get full context.\n>\n> **It records decisions, not reasoning.** For *why* a decision was made, which\n> alternatives were rejected, and what trade-offs were accepted, see\n> `docs/considerations.md`. For the task list, see `docs/work.md`. The\n> assignment brief itself is `docs/business-case.md`.\n\n## 1. Overview\n\nA PoC customer-support conversational AI chatbot (RAG) that answers questions\ngrounded in provided documentation.\n\nContext: a take-home assignment for an AI Solution Engineer interview. The\ndeliverable is a single zip of the whole project, presented in a second\ninterview.\n\nEmphasis, per the brief: code quality, architectural reasoning, AI system\ndesign, and clear explainable decisions \u2014 **not** feature completeness or UI\npolish. Explicitly a PoC, not a production system.\n\n## 2. Goals & Non-Goals\n\n**Goals**\n- Answer support questions grounded in the documentation, with no off-corpus or hallucinated answers\n- Ingest, process and index PDF/DOCX/HTML into a local vector DB\n- Support English and German, including cross-lingual retrieval\n- Demonstrate production-aware thinking (performance, security, observability) without fully building it out\n\n**Non-goals**\n- Full production system or real scalability\n- User authentication\n- Visual/UI polish\n- Streamlit (explicitly disallowed by the brief)\n- AI-usage logging that works for other devs or other AI tools \u2014 PoC-only, Claude Code specific\n\n## 3. Stack\n\n| Layer | Choice |\n|---|---|\n| Backend | FastAPI + uvicorn (async) |\n| Frontend | Plain HTML + vanilla JS, Jinja2 templates, served by the backend |\n| Parsing | Docling, unified across PDF/DOCX/HTML, OCR enabled |\n| Image descriptions | Docling-extracted captions; vision model generates only where absent |\n| Chunking | Docling `HybridChunker`, BGE-M3 tokenizer, ~512 tokens |\n| Embeddings | BGE-M3, local (dense + sparse from one model) |\n| Vector DB | Qdrant, local container |\n| Retrieval | Hybrid dense + sparse, RRF fusion, neighbour expansion. No re-ranking |\n| LLM | Claude Haiku 4.5 (`claude-haiku-4-5`) |\n| Packaging | `docker-compose`: backend + Qdrant, built via `uv` |\n\n**Query pipeline:**\n\n    user message + history\n      \u2192 LLM query rewrite (structured output)\n      \u2192 embed (original + rewritten)\n      \u2192 Qdrant hybrid search (dense + sparse prefetch, RRF)\n      \u2192 neighbour expansion\n      \u2192 Claude generation with citations, streamed to the client\n\n**Ingestion pipeline:**\n\n    document\n      \u2192 Docling parse (layout, tables, images, OCR)\n      \u2192 generate missing image captions, written back into the DoclingDocument\n      \u2192 HybridChunker\n      \u2192 contextualize()\n      \u2192 BGE-M3 embed (dense + sparse)\n      \u2192 Qdrant upsert\n\nOrder matters in ingestion: caption generation must precede chunking.\n\n## 4. Scope\n\n- **Languages:** English and German\n- **Formats:** PDF, DOCX, HTML\n- **Content:** unstructured text, mixed layouts, embedded tables and images\n\n### Corpus\n\n- 1\u201325 pages per document. 50 initial documents, then a batch of 5, then a batch\n  of 10, added on top of the already-indexed set to demonstrate incremental\n  ingestion\n- Generated by us, not provided. ~50/50 EN/DE, with ~20% of content expressed in\n  both languages to exercise cross-lingual retrieval\n- Digital-native \u2014 no scans. Scanned-document handling is a documented\n  limitation, not a supported case\n- **Deliberately hard**, to avoid the corpus becoming an exam we set ourselves:\n  - multi-column and mixed-layout pages\n  - tables spanning page breaks, and unruled tables as well as ruled\n  - charts and diagrams that exist **only as images**, with information found\n    nowhere in the surrounding text\n  - most figures with a visible caption; a deliberate portion with none, so the\n    generated-caption path is exercised\n  - documents long enough to stress chunking and section context\n\n### Design target\n\nThe ingestion/indexing/retrieval design should scale well beyond 65 documents.\nThat is for showcasing production-aware thinking, not because the PoC needs it.\n\n## 5. Architecture\n\n- Backend holds all AI, retrieval and orchestration logic; a frontend UI\n  connects to it (required shape, per brief)\n- Vector DB must be local, not a managed cloud service\n- **Two containers** via `docker-compose` \u2014 backend (uvicorn + FastAPI, also\n  serving the frontend) and Qdrant. No load balancer. `docker compose up` must\n  produce a working app with no manual DB setup\n- **Current state:** minimal FastAPI app (`app/main.py`, `app/routers/`) serving\n  the chat UI, with mocked retrieval and completion (`app/rag/`). Real RAG not\n  yet wired in\n\n## 6. Functional Requirements\n\n### 6.1 Ingestion\n\n- Must ingest PDF, DOCX and HTML containing unstructured text, mixed layouts,\n  tables and images\n- **Parser:** Docling, one unified parser for all three formats, producing a\n  structured document tree with page and section provenance\n- **OCR:** enabled \u2014 it reads text rendered *inside* images (chart axis labels,\n  legends, numbers in diagrams), which the captioner does not reliably transcribe\n- **Image descriptions:** use `PictureItem.captions` where Docling found a\n  visible caption; generate a description with a vision model only where absent.\n  Applies to all three formats. Accessibility metadata (HTML `alt`, DOCX\n  `descr`, PDF `/Alt`) is out of scope \u2014 Docling discards it\n  - captioner: Docling's default picture-description model to start\n  - generation runs as **our own pipeline stage**, because Docling's built-in\n    picture description covers PDF only\n  - record provenance (`extracted` / `generated`) per description\n  - cache generated captions by image hash\n- **Incremental ingestion:** adding documents must not reprocess the corpus.\n  Content-hash manifest per document; deterministic chunk IDs allow upserts\n\n### 6.2 Storage & Retrieval\n\n- **Chunking:** Docling `HybridChunker` with BGE-M3's tokenizer, `max_tokens`\n  ~512, table headers repeated on overflow. Embed `contextualize()` output\n  (heading path + captions); store raw text separately for display\n- **Embeddings:** BGE-M3, run locally, emitting dense and sparse vectors\n- **Vector DB:** Qdrant, hybrid search via the Query API (`prefetch` dense +\n  sparse, RRF fusion)\n  - point IDs must be unsigned ints or UUIDs \u2014 use `uuid5(doc_id, chunk_index)`\n  - unit tests use Qdrant's embedded mode, no Docker required\n- **Neighbour expansion:** retrieve on the chunk, send the chunk plus adjacent\n  siblings to the LLM. Fetch siblings by payload filter on (`doc_id`,\n  `chunk_index` range); merge overlapping windows; clamp to `parent_id`\n- **Re-ranking:** not in the initial build\n- **Chunk payload:** `doc_id`, `chunk_index`, `parent_id`, `page_no(s)`,\n  `heading_path`, `element_types`, `lang`, `source_format`, `caption_provenance`,\n  `doc_content_hash`\n\n### 6.3 Generation\n\n- LLM integrated via API \u2014 **Claude Haiku 4.5** (`claude-haiku-4-5`)\n- Responses use Claude's native **citations**, so answers carry verifiable\n  provenance back to source chunks\n- **Model API constraints on Haiku 4.5:**\n  - `output_config.effort` errors \u2014 not available as a latency dial\n  - thinking is `{\"type\": \"enabled\", \"budget_tokens\": N}` or `\"disabled\"`;\n    adaptive thinking is unavailable\n  - `temperature` is accepted (it 400s on Opus 5 / Sonnet 5)\n  - context window 200K\n  - prompt-cache minimum is 4096 tokens \u2014 caching can silently no-op below that\n- Model configuration must live in one place; upgrading is not a string swap\n- **Guardrails (mandatory):** injection protection, clear system instructions and\n  role separation, and constraints keeping answers within the knowledge base\n- **Guardrail scope: user input only.** Indexed documents are considered safe for\n  the PoC. In production they would be checked\n- Must deliver high accuracy, consistent behaviour and predictable response quality\n\n### 6.4 Backend\n\n- Holds all AI, retrieval and orchestration logic\n- **Conversation history is stateless** \u2014 the client sends prior turns; no\n  server-side session store\n  - the entire history is user input, including assistant turns, which a caller\n    can forge. Validation and guardrails apply to every turn\n  - cap turn count and total length\n- The LLM sees prior turns but **only the current turn's retrieved chunks**\n- Prompt order: system \u2192 history \u2192 retrieved documents \u2192 question\n- **Query rewriting runs on every request**, before retrieval, using structured\n  output. Retrieval runs on both the original and rewritten query as separate\n  `prefetch` branches, fused with RRF\n- Must support concurrent access, stream responses, and emit timing metrics\n\n### 6.5 Frontend\n\n- Any HTML-rendering technology; Streamlit disallowed. Simple chat interface,\n  streaming responses, minimal styling\n- **Implemented:** plain HTML + vanilla JS (`app/templates/index.html`,\n  `app/static/`), no build step, served by the backend. Elements use accessible\n  names and roles for stable e2e targeting\n\n## 7. Non-Functional Requirements\n\n### 7.1 Quality\n\nHigh answer accuracy, consistent and reliable behaviour, predictable response\nquality. No specific metric mandated by the brief.\n\n### 7.2 Performance\n\n- Concurrent multi-user access\n- A single completion should not exceed 5s under normal conditions\n- **Both time-to-first-token and total completion are measured and presented.**\n  TTFT is not cheap here \u2014 the unconditional query rewrite means first token\n  only arrives after rewrite \u2192 embed \u2192 search \u2192 expansion \u2192 generation\n- **Per-stage timers** on rewrite, embed, search, neighbour expansion and\n  generation, so latency can be presented as a breakdown rather than a single\n  number\n\n### 7.3 Security\n\n- Full authentication not required\n- No hardcoded credentials. **Secrets via `.env`**, read into the OS environment\n  and consumed from there. `.env` gitignored, `.env.example` committed\n- Guardrails apply to user input only (see \u00a76.3)\n\n## 8. Testing\n\n- No methodology mandated by the brief; this is our call\n- **Implemented \u2014 e2e:** Playwright (`pytest-playwright`) in `tests/e2e/`,\n  against the real app via a `live_server` fixture. Locators use accessible\n  roles/names/text only, never ids or classes. Not baked into the app image \u2014\n  see `docs/considerations.md` for install and run steps\n- **Performance:** single-prompt latency tests asserting under 5s, plus a\n  lightweight concurrent-users test. No full load-testing harness\n- **Multi-turn is a nice-to-test, not a target.** The golden Q&A set is\n  single-turn; multi-turn cases only if time allows\n\n## 9. Deployment / Packaging\n\n- Deliverable is a **single zip** of the project; no live deployment target\n- `docker-compose.yml` with backend + Qdrant, so one command gives a working app\n  with no manual DB configuration\n- `uv` for local dev; the Docker image builds via `uv` for consistency\n\n## 10. Documentation & AI Usage Transparency\n\nMandatory deliverables: architecture overview, key design decisions and\ntrade-offs, and the chosen models/tools/technologies \u2014 all covered by\n`docs/considerations.md` alongside this document.\n\nAn AI assistant must be used during implementation, and all prompts and relevant\ninteraction traces must be included. `scripts/export_chat_log.py` +\n`.githooks/pre-commit` auto-export Claude Code transcripts into `chat-logs/` per\ncommit. **Known limitation:** this captures Claude Code sessions only, not a\ngeneral multi-tool or multi-dev trace capture.\n\n## 11. Open Questions\n\n- **How to demonstrate and measure accuracy and reliability.** This is the\n  trigger for four deferred decisions: whether re-ranking returns (\u00a76.2),\n  whether the captioner needs upgrading (\u00a76.1), whether Haiku 4.5 is sufficient\n  (\u00a76.3), and whether BGE-M3 earns its size. It is also the detection mechanism\n  for the deferred Docling verification spikes, which fail silently rather than\n  with errors.\n- **Concurrency model.** The embedder and any local vision model are CPU-bound\n  and synchronous; called directly from an async handler they serialise\n  requests. Needs a thread pool with a bounded semaphore, or a separate process.\n\n### Resolved\n\nArchitecture shape (no load balancer, \u00a75) \u00b7 Docker vs plain zip (\u00a79) \u00b7 frontend\napproach (\u00a76.5) \u00b7 Claude-Code-only trace capture (\u00a710) \u00b7 corpus size and source\n(\u00a74) \u00b7 load-testing scope (\u00a78) \u00b7 parsing approach (\u00a76.1) \u00b7 image descriptions\n(\u00a76.1) \u00b7 verification spikes deferred (\u00a711 above) \u00b7 embedding model (\u00a76.2) \u00b7\nvector DB (\u00a76.2) \u00b7 chunking and expansion strategy (\u00a76.2) \u00b7 re-ranking deferred\n(\u00a76.2) \u00b7 LLM choice (\u00a76.3) \u00b7 guardrail scope (\u00a76.3) \u00b7 secrets handling (\u00a77.3) \u00b7\nconversation history (\u00a76.4) \u00b7 latency metrics (\u00a77.2)\n\nRationale for each is in `docs/considerations.md`.\n"
```json
}
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/sebi/workspace/sebi/chat-bot/docs/considerations.md",
  "content": "# Considerations\n\n> **What this document is.** The development narrative \u2014 the decisions taken,\n> why they were taken, what was rejected and what trade-offs were accepted. This\n> is the document intended for presentation.\n>\n> It is the *why* behind `docs/requirements.md`, which records the *what*.\n> Section references point back to it. Tasks live in `docs/work.md`.\n\nA recurring policy runs through most of these decisions: **take the cheapest\noption that could work, and upgrade only on measured evidence.** It applies to\nthe embedding model, the image captioner and the LLM. The cost of that policy is\nthat it requires an evaluation harness to be actionable \u2014 which is why the\naccuracy question is the last open one and has the most riding on it.\n\n## Scope\n\nJust a POC.\n\nNot having any initial data makes it harder to ensure the solution addresses\nall cases.\n\n## Architecture\n\nUse uvicorn + fastapi for speed (async).\n\nOriginal sketch:\n\n    Nginx/AWS load balancer ->((server docker): uvicorn -> fastapi app )-> ( (db docker) vector db)\n\n**Superseded:** the Nginx/load-balancer tier was dropped as unnecessary for a\nPoC. The shipped shape is two containers via `docker-compose` \u2014 backend\n(uvicorn + FastAPI, also serving the frontend) and the vector DB \u2014 with no LB\nin front. Priority is a smooth install for another dev: `docker compose up`\nshould bring up the app with the vector DB already configured (requirements \u00a75).\n\n## Document corpus\n\n### Generating the corpus \u2014 the self-set-exam trap\n\nWe author both the corpus and the pipeline that reads it. The obvious failure\nmode is generating documents that happen to be easy for our own parser, which\nmakes the pipeline look good and proves nothing. The brief explicitly asks for\n\"mixed layouts\" and \"embedded information that requires careful processing\", so\nthe corpus has to be *deliberately* hard \u2014 multi-column pages, unruled tables,\ntables spanning page breaks, charts that exist only as images, and a portion of\nfigures with no visible caption so the generated-caption path actually fires.\nThe full specification is in requirements \u00a74.\n\nThe same principle applies to the golden Q&A set: questions whose answers live\nonly in a table or only in a chart image are the ones that prove the pipeline\nworks.\n\n## Document parsing\n\n### Docling, unified across all three formats\n\nAlternatives considered: best-of-breed per format (pdfplumber + python-docx +\nBeautifulSoup), other unified parsers (Unstructured, Marker, MinerU), cloud\ndocument-AI (LlamaParse, Azure Document Intelligence, Textract), and\nLLM-as-parser (render pages to images, send to Claude vision).\n\n- **Cloud document-AI \u2014 rejected.** Conflicts with the local/reproducible\n  posture and adds another vendor and API key.\n- **Per-format libraries \u2014 rejected.** Genuinely defensible: DOCX and HTML\n  already carry table structure natively, so ML parsing buys nothing there, and\n  PDF is the only hard format. But it means three parsers, three sets of edge\n  cases and three things to explain, all behind a hand-written common\n  interface. Also worth noting on licensing: PyMuPDF, the fastest PDF option,\n  is AGPL \u2014 awkward for a submission. pdfplumber (MIT) would have been the\n  pick.\n- **Docling \u2014 chosen.** One library and one output model for all three formats.\n  Critically, it emits a **structured document tree** (headings, paragraphs,\n  tables, images, with page and section provenance) rather than a flat Markdown\n  string \u2014 that provenance is what makes table-aware chunking and page-level\n  citations possible. Layout analysis plus dedicated table-structure\n  recognition covers the PDF case. MIT licensed.\n- **Trade-off accepted:** ships ML models, so a bigger image and slower ingest.\n  Ingest is offline, so it never touches the 5s completion target.\n\nDocling's `HybridChunker` also turned out to settle most of the chunking\ndecision \u2014 see below.\n\n### OCR \u2014 kept on\n\nThe corpus is digital-native, so OCR isn't needed for page text and switching it\noff would be a free speedup. Kept on anyway because OCR reads text rendered\n*inside* images \u2014 chart axis labels, legends, numbers baked into diagrams \u2014\nwhich the captioner does not reliably transcribe. The two complement each other\non exactly the figures that matter.\n\n### Images \u2014 caption-first, generate only the gaps\n\nGoal: every image ends up with an indexed, referenceable text description. Use\nthe caption the document already carries; only generate one when there isn't\none. The source of an existing description is **`PictureItem.captions`** \u2014 the\nvisible \"Figure N: \u2026\" text Docling's layout analysis associates with the figure.\nThis works across all three formats and needs no extra code.\n\n**Accessibility metadata is deliberately out of scope.** An earlier version of\nthis design was alt-text-first: prefer HTML `alt` / DOCX `descr` / PDF `/Alt`,\ngenerate only when absent. Research into Docling killed it:\n\n- Docling's HTML backend never reads the `alt` attribute (it handles `href`,\n  `hidden`, `aria-hidden`, `style`, `id`, `class`, `start` \u2014 not `alt`), and its\n  DOCX backend never reads `docPr`/`descr`.\n- `PictureItem` has no field to hold such a value anyway. Its fields are\n  `label`, `annotations`, `image`, `prov`, plus `captions` / `references` /\n  `footnotes` inherited from `FloatingItem`. Only `captions` and VLM\n  `annotations` carry text.\n\nGetting alt text would therefore mean parsing each source file a second time\nand joining to Docling's pictures on image content hash. Not worth the\ncomplexity for a PoC, especially since real technical documentation usually\ncaptions its figures visibly \u2014 which is the signal we actually want.\n\n**A second Docling constraint shapes the design:** its built-in\npicture-description enrichment appears to run for PDF only, not DOCX/PPTX\n([docling#2225](https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2225)) or\nstandalone images\n([docling#2446](https://github.com/docling-project/docling/issues/2446)). So\ncaption *generation* is our own pipeline stage over the images Docling extracts,\nrather than Docling's enrichment pipeline. Upside: the captioner model becomes a\nfree choice instead of one constrained by Docling's OpenAI-shaped API\nintegration.\n\n- **Provenance:** record `extracted` vs `generated` per description. Cheap, and\n  it lets the evaluation answer whether generated captions actually help\n  retrieval or just add noise \u2014 the evidence that would justify upgrading the\n  captioner.\n- **Model:** start with Docling's default picture-description model. It is small\n  and its captions are generic (\"a bar chart\") rather than factual (\"return\n  rates by product category, electronics highest at 12%\"), and only the latter\n  is retrievable. Accepted for now under the upgrade-on-evidence policy. The\n  caption-first design also means it only runs on the minority of images that\n  lack a visible caption.\n- **Cache captions by image hash.** A few hundred images at CPU vision-model\n  speeds is plausibly 30\u201360 minutes of ingest; re-running that every iteration\n  would make development miserable, and hash-keyed caching fits the incremental\n  ingestion design anyway.\n\n### Image size\n\nModel weights stack up, and a slow `docker compose up` works directly against\nthe smooth-install priority in requirements \u00a75:\n\n    BGE-M3                        ~2GB\n    Docling layout + table models ~0.5GB\n    OCR engine                    small\n    picture-description VLM       0.5GB (default) .. 6GB (larger local model)\n    torch (CPU)                   ~1GB\n\nMitigation regardless of the final numbers: bake model weights into the image at\nbuild time so first run is fully offline and doesn't download at startup.\n\n## Retrieval stack\n\n### Vector DB \u2014 Qdrant\n\nCandidates narrowed to Qdrant and Weaviate: both ship an official Docker image\nand both advertise hybrid search. Chroma was dropped once BGE-M3 was chosen,\nbecause the sparse-vector requirement rules it out.\n\n**The decisive difference is what \"hybrid\" means in each product.**\n\n- **Weaviate** hybrid = dense vector (bring your own) fused with **Weaviate's own\n  internal BM25F** inverted index over object properties. The `hybrid()` query\n  takes `query`, `vector`, `alpha`, `bm25_operator`, `query_properties` \u2014 there\n  is no parameter for a precomputed sparse vector. You cannot feed it BGE-M3's\n  sparse output.\n- **Qdrant** treats sparse vectors as a first-class type, and the Query API\n  fuses dense + sparse server-side in a single request via `prefetch` with RRF\n  or DBSF.\n\nSince BGE-M3 was chosen precisely *because* it emits dense and sparse from one\nmodel, Weaviate would mean throwing half of it away \u2014 and with it the\njustification for picking BGE-M3 at all.\n\nBeyond that:\n\n- **German breaks BM25.** Lexical matching works on surface forms, so a query\n  for *R\u00fccksendung* does not match *R\u00fccksendebedingungen* without configured\n  stemming and decompounding. Learned sparse vectors from a multilingual model\n  match on semantics instead. With EN/DE as a hard requirement this is a real\n  quality difference, not a theoretical one.\n- **Embedded mode.** Qdrant runs in-process against `:memory:` or a local path,\n  so unit tests can exercise real retrieval without Docker \u2014 the same instinct\n  that kept Playwright out of the app image.\n- **Named vectors** let a second embedding model live alongside the first in one\n  collection, turning the possible BGE-M3-vs-smaller-model comparison into a\n  re-query rather than a second ingest.\n- Lighter: Rust single binary, smaller image, lower memory than Weaviate's Go\n  runtime \u2014 relevant given how much the model weights already cost us.\n\n**In fairness to Weaviate:** it is the more complete product \u2014 multi-tenancy,\nRBAC, replication, built-in vectorizer modules \u2014 and its single `alpha` knob is\nmore ergonomic than composing prefetch clauses. None of that is load-bearing for\na single-user PoC over 65 documents, and it is more surface area to configure\nand to explain.\n\n**Gotcha to design around:** Qdrant point IDs must be unsigned integers or\nUUIDs, not arbitrary strings. Deterministic chunk IDs therefore need hashing \u2014\n`uuid5` over (`doc_id`, `chunk_index`) stays deterministic, which is what makes\nupsert-based incremental ingestion work.\n\n**Worth stating explicitly: the DB and embedder decisions are coupled.**\nWeaviate + BGE-M3 is the one combination that doesn't hang together. A coherent\nWeaviate stack would be Weaviate + its BM25 + a dense-only embedder \u2014 at which\npoint e5-small becomes attractive again at a quarter of the size, and the\nembedding decision below would need reopening.\n\n### Embedding model \u2014 BGE-M3\n\nOptions considered: `multilingual-e5-small`, `multilingual-e5-large`, BGE-M3,\nCohere multilingual embeddings.\n\n- **Cohere \u2014 excluded.** Free-tier rate limits make a reviewer's\n  `docker compose up` unreproducible, and it adds a second API vendor plus\n  another key to manage.\n- **e5-small \u2014 considered, then rejected.** The initial instinct was the usual\n  upgrade-on-evidence policy: start with the smallest model, which also keeps\n  image size down. Rejected because it only covers the *dense* half of\n  retrieval \u2014 pairing it with a separate BM25/lexical component to get hybrid\n  search means more moving parts to build and maintain.\n- **BGE-M3 \u2014 chosen.** Emits dense **and** sparse vectors from a single model,\n  so hybrid retrieval works out of the box and there is no second lexical path\n  to hand-roll. Multilingual and cross-lingual by design, which is what the ~20%\n  EN/DE content overlap in the corpus is meant to exercise. 8k token input\n  limit, so chunk size is not constrained by the embedder (e5's 512-token\n  ceiling would have capped it).\n- **Trade-off accepted:** ~2GB of weights and CPU inference, versus ~470MB for\n  e5-small. Judged worth it for the code simplification.\n\nThis is the one place the upgrade-on-evidence policy was overridden \u2014 and the\nreason was code simplicity rather than quality.\n\nNote for a possible later comparison: switching embedder changes the vector\ndimension (BGE-M3 is 1024-dim), which means recreating the Qdrant collection\nrather than migrating it in place. Keeping the embedder behind an interface and\nrecording model name + dimension in the ingest manifest makes that swap cheap;\nQdrant's named vectors would allow A/B-ing two embedders over one ingest.\n\n### Chunking \u2014 HybridChunker, ~512 tokens, neighbour expansion\n\nMost of this decision turned out to be already solved by Docling, so the design\nis mostly about *not* rebuilding what the chunker does: a tokenizer parameter\n(point it at BGE-M3's own tokenizer so token counts are real rather than\nestimated), two-pass split-then-merge on structural boundaries, table headers\nrepeated on overflow instead of truncation, and `contextualize()` which returns\na metadata-enriched serialization for the embedder. We embed that and keep raw\ntext for display. A useful side effect with hybrid retrieval: the heading path\nlands in the **sparse** vector too, so lexical matches on section names work.\n\n**Size \u2014 ~512 tokens.** BGE-M3 accepts 8k, so the model isn't the constraint;\nretrieval quality is. One vector over a long passage averages across topics and\nmatches nothing strongly. Since re-ranking is deferred, retrieval precision\ncarries all the weight, which argues for the smaller end of the useful range.\n\n**Retrieval unit \u2260 generation unit.** Small chunks retrieve precisely but hand\nthe model fragments; large chunks ground well but retrieve poorly.\n\n| Approach | Trade-off |\n|---|---|\n| Same unit for both | Simplest; fragments may lack context |\n| **Neighbour expansion \u2014 chosen** | Retrieve the chunk, send it plus adjacent siblings. No side-store, predictable token cost |\n| Parent-section expansion | Best grounding, but needs parent text stored outside the vector payload, and one large section can blow the latency budget |\n\nParent expansion was the initial choice and then dropped: it requires keeping\nsection text in a side store (storing it per-chunk would duplicate a section\nonce per child), plus a size cap and a fallback for oversized sections.\nNeighbour expansion gets most of the grounding benefit with none of that, since\nneighbours are just other chunks already in Qdrant, fetched by a payload filter\non (`doc_id`, `chunk_index` range) \u2014 no extra embedding work at query time. It\ndoes need overlapping windows merged, and the window clamped to `parent_id` so a\nneighbour from a different section can't mislead grounding.\n\nThis composes well with Claude's native citations: citations resolve to spans\n*within* whatever block is sent, so an expanded window costs no attribution\nprecision. Broad grounding context and narrow attribution at the same time.\n\n**Sequencing trap:** caption generation has to happen **before** chunking. The\nchunker attaches captions to chunks, so a generated caption must be written back\ninto the `DoclingDocument` first. Get the order wrong and images that had no\nvisible caption produce empty chunks and the generation work is wasted.\n\n### Re-ranking \u2014 deferred\n\nNot in the initial build; revisit only if time allows. Consequence: retrieval\nquality rests entirely on BGE-M3 hybrid search, so the accuracy evaluation is\nwhat tells us whether that is good enough \u2014 it is the trigger for reconsidering.\n\nThis also removed re-ranking as a justification for the staged corpus batches\n(+5, then +10). Those still earn their place by demonstrating incremental\ningestion.\n\n## Generation\n\n### LLM \u2014 Claude Haiku 4.5 to start\n\nUpgrade-on-evidence again: cheapest and fastest first. It is also the best fit\nfor the \u22645s target, and for grounded extractive answering over retrieved context\nthe hard problem is retrieval, not generation \u2014 which is where the complexity\nbudget went.\n\nUpgrade path is Sonnet 5, then Opus 5. Expect the ceiling to show up in\nmulti-hop synthesis across several chunks and in nuanced German phrasing, rather\nthan in simple extraction \u2014 so the golden Q&A set needs both, or the ceiling\nstays invisible.\n\n**Haiku 4.5 is an older-generation model and its request surface differs from\nthe newer ones. This matters because the upgrade is not a model-string swap:**\n\n| | Haiku 4.5 | Sonnet 5 / Opus 5 |\n|---|---|---|\n| Thinking | `{\"type\": \"enabled\", \"budget_tokens\": N}` / `\"disabled\"` | `{\"type\": \"adaptive\"}`; `budget_tokens` \u2192 400 |\n| `output_config.effort` | errors | `low` \u2026 `max` |\n| `temperature` | allowed | \u2192 400 |\n| Context | 200K | 1M |\n| Prompt-cache minimum | 4096 tokens | 512 (Opus 5) |\n\nConsequences:\n\n- The latency dial is **not** `effort` here (it doesn't exist on Haiku) \u2014 it is\n  thinking configuration.\n- `temperature` is available, a genuine lever for the \"predictable response\n  quality\" requirement \u2014 but it disappears on upgrade, so nothing load-bearing\n  should depend on it.\n- **Prompt caching may silently do nothing.** Haiku's 4096-token minimum is high\n  and a system prompt plus guardrails may fall below it. It doesn't error;\n  `cache_creation_input_tokens` just stays 0. Assert `cache_read_input_tokens >\n  0` rather than assuming.\n- Keep model configuration in one place so the swap is a config change, not a\n  hunt through the codebase.\n\n### Citations\n\nClaude's native citations are used on the generation call: retrieved chunks go\nin as `document` blocks with citations enabled, and the response carries spans\ntied back to source chunks. This turns \"high answer accuracy\" from a claim into\na verifiable artifact, and gives the frontend clickable sources for free. It\nalso provides a downstream enforcement point for the knowledge-base guardrail \u2014\nan answer with zero citations can be replaced with the fallback message.\n\nNote citations are incompatible with `output_config.format` within a single\ncall. That is not a conflict here, because the query rewrite (which does use\nstructured output) is a separate request.\n\n### Guardrails \u2014 user input only\n\nInjection defence applies to what the user sends. **Indexed documents are\nconsidered safe for the PoC** and are not defended against. In production they\nwould be checked \u2014 retrieved content is attacker-controllable and would need the\nsame containment as user input, meaning delimited retrieved text and an explicit\ninstruction to treat it as data rather than instructions.\n\nRecorded as a deliberate scope boundary, not an oversight.\n\nOne interaction worth noting: because conversation history is stateless and\nclient-supplied (below), the *history* is user input too \u2014 including assistant\nturns, which a caller can forge. Guardrails therefore apply to every turn, not\njust the newest.\n\n## Orchestration\n\n### Conversation history \u2014 stateless\n\nThe client sends prior turns with each request; there is no server-side session\nstore. A store would mean adding storage (an in-memory dict breaks the moment\nmore than one worker runs) for no PoC benefit, and statelessness keeps\nhorizontal scaling free. The costs are that the whole history must be validated\nand guarded, and that turn count and total length need capping.\n\nThe LLM sees prior turns but **only the current turn's retrieved chunks** \u2014\ncarrying previous turns' documents forward would grow context by roughly one\nretrieval window per turn. Prompt order is system \u2192 history \u2192 retrieved\ndocuments \u2192 question, keeping the volatile part last so the stable prefix stays\ncacheable.\n\n### Query rewriting \u2014 unconditional\n\nA rewrite step runs before retrieval on every request, doing double duty:\nresolving context-dependent follow-ups (\"and what about returns?\") and general\nquery improvement, which was wanted independently.\n\nConditional rewriting \u2014 only when history is non-empty \u2014 was considered, and\nwould have kept first turns free. Rejected because the query-improvement job\napplies to every request, not just follow-ups.\n\n- **Cost:** a second LLM call per request, **serial** and ahead of retrieval, so\n  it lands directly on time-to-first-token.\n- **Risk:** rewriting can drift from user intent and retrieve *worse* than the\n  raw query would have.\n- **Mitigation:** search on **both** the original and rewritten query as separate\n  `prefetch` branches and let RRF fuse them, rather than replacing the original.\n  The extra DB work is negligible next to the LLM call already being paid for.\n- The rewrite call uses **structured outputs** so it reliably returns just a\n  query string.\n\n**Multi-turn is a nice-to-test, not a target.** History is supported, but\nmulti-turn behaviour isn't a graded goal \u2014 the golden Q&A set is single-turn.\nThe consequence to be aware of is that the follow-up path ships largely\nuntested.\n\n## Performance and measurement\n\nBoth **time-to-first-token** and **total completion** are measured and\npresented. The brief's \"5 seconds\" is ambiguous once responses stream, so\nshowing both is the honest answer, and which one gets emphasised depends on what\nthe data supports.\n\n**TTFT is not cheap in this design.** Because the query rewrite is unconditional\nand serial, first token only arrives after rewrite \u2192 embed \u2192 search \u2192 expansion\n\u2192 generation. In a plain chat app TTFT is near-instant; here it carries most of\nthe pipeline. So TTFT is not automatically the friendlier number.\n\nThat is the argument for **per-stage timers** rather than two endpoints. A\nbreakdown showing where the time goes, and which levers move it, is a stronger\nresult than a single headline figure \u2014 and it is the evidence base for three\ndeferred decisions at once: whether re-ranking's cost is affordable, whether the\nrewrite call earns its place, and whether BGE-M3 on CPU is the bottleneck.\n\n**Concurrency note:** the embedder and any local vision model are CPU-bound and\nsynchronous. Called directly from an async handler they block the event loop and\nthe concurrency requirement quietly fails under load. They need a thread pool\nbehind a bounded semaphore, or a separate process.\n\n## Security\n\n**Secrets via `.env`**, read into the OS environment and consumed from there. No\ncredentials in source, satisfying the brief's mandatory requirement. `.env` must\nbe gitignored with a placeholder `.env.example` committed in its place. In\nproduction this would be a secrets manager rather than a file on disk.\n\n## Verification spikes \u2014 deferred\n\nSeveral open questions about Docling's behaviour \u2014 whether `PictureItem.image`\npopulates for DOCX/HTML, whether `HybridChunker` emits pictures as their own\nchunks, whether the PDF-only picture-description limits still hold in the pinned\nversion \u2014 are deliberately **not** investigated up front. We react if problems\nsurface.\n\nThe caveat worth being explicit about: two of these fail **silently**. If\npictures aren't emitted as chunks, nothing throws \u2014 images simply never appear\nin retrieval results. So error monitoring won't catch them; the evaluation will,\nvia the golden questions whose answers exist only inside a chart image. That\nmakes the eval the real safety net for this deferral, which is fine as long as\nit runs before ingestion is considered finished.\n\n## AI usage logging\n\nDid not consider making the ai logging necessary work for another dev or other\nai tools than Claude.\n\nKnown limitation: `scripts/export_chat_log.py` (+ `.githooks/pre-commit`) only\ncaptures Claude Code sessions. It satisfies this PoC's own AI-usage transparency\nrequirement (requirements \u00a710), but it is not a general multi-tool/multi-dev\ntrace capture \u2014 another dev, or a different AI assistant, would need separate\ntooling.\n\n## E2E testing (Playwright)\n\nNot baked into a dev Docker image \u2014 the browser + its system libs (libnspr4,\nlibnss3, etc.) only matter for running e2e tests, not for running/reviewing\nthe app itself, so it'd be dead weight in the main image for this PoC. If\nCI or multi-machine dev parity becomes a real need later, the right shape is\na separate test-only Dockerfile/service, not bundling it into the app image.\n\nTo install and run locally:\n\n    uv sync --group dev                      # installs pytest, pytest-playwright\n    uv run playwright install chromium       # downloads the Chromium browser binary\n    sudo uv run playwright install-deps chromium   # installs required OS shared libs (needs interactive sudo)\n    uv run pytest -m e2e -v\n\nThe `install-deps` step needs interactive sudo (apt-get under the hood), so\nit can't be run non-interactively/by an agent \u2014 a human has to run it once\nper machine. See `.claude/skills/e2e-testing/SKILL.md` for locator\nconventions used in these tests.\n"
}
```

Done. The three docs now have clean, non-overlapping roles.

**`requirements.md`** — project description. Added a **Stack table** and both **pipeline diagrams** near the top, which is the fastest way for a person or agent to get oriented. Decisions are stated as facts; rationale is gone, replaced by a pointer to considerations. Renumbered (§5.x → §6.x) now that Stack is its own section, and the resolved-questions list is compressed to one paragraph of cross-references instead of eleven strikethrough lines.

**`considerations.md`** — the development narrative. Opens by naming the *upgrade-on-evidence* policy that recurs through most decisions, then follows the shape of the build. Absorbed the rationale that had been duplicated in requirements, and picked up decisions that previously had no "why" recorded anywhere: citations, guardrail scope, history, query rewriting, latency measurement, secrets.

**`work.md`** — bare task list. All "resolved: X because Y" annotations removed; resolved *decide* tasks deleted outright since the decision record lives elsewhere.

## Divergences fixed

- §5.2 was headed "parent expansion" while its body described neighbour expansion
- §10 still resolved image handling as "alt-text-first with a vision-model fallback" — superseded by caption-first
- A stale "Open:" line saying the sparse requirement "narrows §10's vector DB question", after the DB was chosen
- §7 duplicated §10's accuracy question as a second open item
- "To verify" blocks in considerations duplicated the deferred-spikes section
- The parsing section still called `HybridChunker` a bonus that "likely settles the chunking decision" after it had settled it

## Tasks I added while tidying

Things that were decided in conversation but never made it to a task: the `retrieve()` signature change, the thread-pool/semaphore for CPU-bound models, generating the golden Q&A set from the corpus generator, enabling citations with a zero-citation fallback, baking model weights into the image, and sending prior turns from the frontend.

## One file left

**`docs/stack-proposal.md`** is now the only source of divergence remaining — it still recommends e5-small sizing, a local cross-encoder reranker, and alt-text handling. Every idea in it that survived is recorded in the other two docs. I'd delete it, but it's your call — say the word and I will.
